## Lesson 19 — 1985-1991: the bloc that dissolved

Progress: 19 / 32 | Stage: Core | Block C — the role of the power blocs

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §9's trap entry, that the chapter's bloc material is book-period and must not be read as a description of the present; with the Advanced companion §12's factual-risk check on the same point, read against PDF pp.213-214 (printed 196-197), which supply the 1985 reforms and their four named contents, the 1991 collapse, the Berlin Wall, the redundancy of the Warsaw Pact and the chapter's present-tense claim about the surviving alliance.
CA search: "NATO membership current number of member countries accession since 2009"
CA found: 📰 **Sweden deposited its instrument of accession to the North Atlantic Treaty on 7 March 2024, becoming the alliance's 32nd member country; Finland had done so on 4 April 2023 as the 31st** — the alliance's own official membership record. ⚠️ This is the single dated anchor carried anywhere in this file, and it is used only as a status check on the chapter's own present-tense sentence. ❌ No doctrine, argument, practice item or verdict in this file depends on it.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The passage, and its five stages

✅ Neither canonical owner reproduces any of this, and it is the hinge between the chapter's second and third divisions:

> However, since the early 1990s there has been a radical change in the position of the power blocs. The reforms initiated by Mikhail Gorbachev in the Soviet Union in 1985 encouraged free flow of information, social and political debates, decentralization of economy and review of foreign policy in the communist camp. This led to the relaxation of the Soviet Union's control over the communist nations of Eastern Europe, and eventual collapse of the communist system in most of these countries by 1991. This marked the dissolution of the one power bloc led by the former USSR and consequent end of the 'cold war'. The Wall of Berlin between East and West Germany was demolished, and Warsaw Pact also became redundant. However, the North Atlantic Treaty Organization (NATO) is still going strong. From military angle, only one power bloc is left now.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.9, PDF pp.213-214 (printed 196-197)

```text
   1985   REFORMS initiated by MIKHAIL GORBACHEV in the Soviet Union
            four named contents:
              (i)   FREE FLOW OF INFORMATION
              (ii)  SOCIAL AND POLITICAL DEBATES
              (iii) DECENTRALIZATION OF ECONOMY
              (iv)  REVIEW OF FOREIGN POLICY
                        |
   THEN   "RELAXATION of the Soviet Union's CONTROL over the communist
          nations of Eastern Europe"
                        |
   BY 1991 "eventual COLLAPSE of the communist system in most of these
           countries"
                        |
   RESULT  (a) "DISSOLUTION of the ONE power bloc led by the former USSR"
           (b) "consequent END of the 'cold war'"
           (c) "The Wall of Berlin between East and West Germany was
                DEMOLISHED"
           (d) "WARSAW PACT also became REDUNDANT"
           (e) "However, the North Atlantic Treaty Organization (NATO)
                is STILL GOING STRONG"
                        |
   VERDICT "From MILITARY angle, ONLY ONE power bloc is LEFT NOW"
```

⚠️ The four named reform contents are worth holding as a set, because they are of two kinds: two are about *openness* — free flow of information, and social and political debates — and two are about *restructuring* — decentralization of the economy, and review of foreign policy. 🔑 The chapter's causal claim is that openness plus restructuring inside the leading state produced relaxation of control over the camp, and relaxation of control produced collapse.

### 2. The asymmetry that sets up Part III

⚠️ The decisive observation is not that one bloc dissolved but that the other did not.

| ⚠️ | **Warsaw Pact** | **NATO** |
|---|---|---|
| Status after 1991 | ✅ **Became redundant** | ✅ **Is still going strong** |
| Bloc it led | ✅ **Dissolution of the one power bloc led by the former USSR** | ⚠️ Not dissolved |
| Net result | 🔑 **From military angle, only one power bloc is left now** | |

⚠️ A reader expecting the chapter to celebrate the end of bipolar constraint is immediately checked. ⚠️ The chapter's next sentence does not say that sovereignty was restored; it says that **the substantive base of domination in the contemporary world has been shifting from military power to economic power**. 🔑 Domination is treated as a constant whose base changes — which is exactly the hinge argument identified at Lesson 5.

⚠️ So the end of the Cold War, in this chapter, is not a liberation. ⚠️ It is the moment at which one form of constraint is replaced by another and the subject changes from blocs to globalization.

### 3. 📰 One dated status check, and its strict limits

⚠️ The chapter makes a present-tense claim — **the North Atlantic Treaty Organization (NATO) is still going strong** — inside a **2009** edition. ⚠️ A present-tense claim invites exactly one legitimate question: is it still true at the date of reading? ⚠️ That question is answered here once, and nowhere else in this file.

📰 On the alliance's own official membership record, **Finland deposited its instrument of accession to the North Atlantic Treaty on 4 April 2023, becoming the 31st member country**, and **Sweden deposited its instrument of accession on 7 March 2024, becoming the 32nd member country**.

⚠️ What this does and does not establish:

- ✅ It establishes that the chapter's 2009 sentence has not been falsified by subsequent dissolution: the organisation has continued and has admitted new members.
- ❌ It does not establish anything about the chapter's wider thesis. ❌ Membership size is not evidence about domination, autonomy, interdependence or sovereignty.
- ❌ It is not a globalisation claim and is not used as one. ➡️ The contemporary international order, alliance politics and foreign policy belong to the **International Relations** owners.
- ❌ It introduces no second dated anchor anywhere in this file; every other lesson records a truthful nil in its pre-teach checklist.
- ⚠️ It is a status check on one sentence, carried at the level of precision stated, and no doctrine, concept check, Mains model or application anchor in this file depends on it.

⚠️ Note separately, from Lesson 14, the bounded historical cross-check on the alliance's **4 April 1949** signature by twelve states and the Federal Republic of Germany's accession on **6 May 1955**. ⚠️ That is a historical verification of a list, not a current-affairs anchor, and the two should not be conflated.

### 4. How to use this material without dating errors

⚠️ The two canonical owners are unusually emphatic here and their instruction should be followed exactly: ❌ do **not** present NATO, the Warsaw Pact, the third world, non-alignment or the end of the Cold War as unmarked current affairs; ✅ they are **dated historical or book-period illustrations** from a **2009** text.

⚠️ A reliable formula for answer writing:

```text
   SAFE      "Writing in 2009, Gauba records that the Warsaw Pact had
             become redundant while NATO continued, leaving -- 'from
             military angle' -- only one power bloc."
                        |
   ALSO SAFE "The chapter's point is structural: the base of
             domination shifts from military to economic power."
                        |
   UNSAFE    Any present-tense sentence about alliances, membership,
             strategic balance or the current international order
             that is attributed to the chapter.
```

### 5. Criticism, reply, residual

⚠️ **Criticism.** ⚠️ The causal chain is thin for an event of this size: four reform contents inside one state are made to explain the collapse of a system across many, with no account of the internal politics of the Eastern European countries themselves, no economic data, and no consideration of alternative explanations.

⚠️ **Reply.** ⚠️ The chapter is a theory text and is not offering a history of 1989. ⚠️ Its chain is sufficient for its purpose, which is to establish one proposition: that the bipolar constraint ended for reasons internal to one bloc rather than through any success of the states that bore the constraint. 🔑 That matters because it means the non-aligned did not defeat the bloc system; the bloc system ended, and they continued.

⚠️ **Residual.** ⚠️ The chapter's **only one power bloc is left now** is restricted by its own qualifier, **from military angle**, and the chapter never says what follows from that restriction. ⚠️ If domination has shifted to an economic base, the count of military blocs may no longer be the relevant measure at all — and the chapter moves to Part III without resolving the question it has just raised.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Since the early 1990s there has been a radical change in the position of the power blocs.** (PDF p.213)
2. ✅ **Reforms initiated by Mikhail Gorbachev in the Soviet Union in 1985.**
3. 🔑 Four named contents: **free flow of information**, **social and political debates**, **decentralization of economy**, **review of foreign policy**.
4. ✅ These led to **relaxation of the Soviet Union's control over the communist nations of Eastern Europe**.
5. ✅ And to **eventual collapse of the communist system in most of these countries by 1991**.
6. ✅ This marked **the dissolution of the one power bloc led by the former USSR and consequent end of the 'cold war'**.
7. ✅ **The Wall of Berlin between East and West Germany was demolished, and Warsaw Pact also became redundant.**
8. ✅ **However, the North Atlantic Treaty Organization (NATO) is still going strong.**
9. 🔑 **From military angle, only one power bloc is left now** — ⚠️ note the qualifier.
10. 📰 Dated status check, carried once in this file: **Finland acceded on 4 April 2023 as the 31st member; Sweden on 7 March 2024 as the 32nd**, on the alliance's own official record. ❌ Nothing in this file depends on it.
11. ⚠️ The end of the Cold War is not presented as liberation: the chapter's next move is that the **substantive base of domination... has been shifting from military power to economic power**.
12. ⚠️ Objection: the causal chain is thin; ⚠️ reply: it suffices to establish that the constraint ended for reasons internal to one bloc, not through the non-aligned states' success; ⚠️ residual: the **from military angle** qualifier is never cashed out.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why does the chapter follow the dissolution of one power bloc with a statement about the shifting base of domination rather than with a statement about restored sovereignty?

**Model answer:** ⚠️ Because the division's subject is constraint, and the chapter's evidence is that constraint did not end. ✅ The collapse is reported precisely: reforms of **1985** produced **relaxation of the Soviet Union's control**, then **eventual collapse of the communist system in most of these countries by 1991**, then **the dissolution of the one power bloc led by the former USSR and consequent end of the 'cold war'**, with the **Warsaw Pact** becoming **redundant**. ⚠️ But the chapter immediately records an asymmetry — **the North Atlantic Treaty Organization (NATO) is still going strong**, so that **from military angle, only one power bloc is left now** — and then says that **the substantive base of domination in the contemporary world has been shifting from military power to economic power, spread over various centres**. 🔑 Domination is the constant; its base is the variable. ⚠️ Had the chapter announced restored sovereignty, it could not have gone on to call globalization **a new challenge to the sovereignty of the nation-state** in the very next sentence.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not write that the chapter treats 1991 as the end of external challenges to sovereignty. ⚠️ It treats 1991 as a change of instrument. ⚠️ And ❌ do not carry any of this material into the present tense: these are **2009**-edition statements, and the only dated check in this file concerns the continued existence of the surviving alliance, not the balance of power.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [15]:** Trace the dissolution of the bipolar order as Gauba records it, and explain why he treats it as a change of instrument rather than as a restoration of sovereignty. *(Trace and explain — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ The sequence begins in **1985**, with **reforms initiated by Mikhail Gorbachev in the Soviet Union** that encouraged **free flow of information, social and political debates, decentralization of economy and review of foreign policy in the communist camp**. ✅ These produced **relaxation of the Soviet Union's control over the communist nations of Eastern Europe**, and **eventual collapse of the communist system in most of these countries by 1991**. ✅ That marked **the dissolution of the one power bloc led by the former USSR and consequent end of the 'cold war'**; **the Wall of Berlin between East and West Germany was demolished, and Warsaw Pact also became redundant**. ✅ Crucially, the other alliance did not dissolve: **the North Atlantic Treaty Organization (NATO) is still going strong**, so that **from military angle, only one power bloc is left now**.

⚠️ The chapter treats this as a change of instrument for two reasons in its own text. ⚠️ First, asymmetry: one bloc ended and the other did not, so the structure was simplified, not dismantled. ✅ Second, decisively, the next sentence states that **the substantive base of domination in the contemporary world has been shifting from military power to economic power, spread over various centres**, with superior technology spreading **environmental pollution all over the world** and **linking of all parts of the world by an effective communication network**. ✅ Hence **the process of 'globalization' has emerged as a new challenge to the sovereignty of the nation-state**. 🔑 Domination persists; only its base moves.

**Rubric [15]:** the 1985 reforms with at least three of their four named contents **3** · relaxation of control and the 1991 collapse **2** · dissolution of the bloc and end of the Cold War **2** · the Berlin Wall and Warsaw Pact redundancy **1** · the NATO asymmetry with the **from military angle** qualifier **2** · the shift from military to economic base quoted or closely reported **3** · the explicit inference that this is a change of instrument, not a restoration **2**.

## Lesson 20 — The hand-off: from military power to economic power

Progress: 20 / 32 | Stage: Core | Block C — the role of the power blocs

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §4's third spine step, that economic interdependence, communication networks and environmental spillovers make absolute external sovereignty harder to sustain; with the Advanced companion §3's final step, read against PDF p.214 (printed 197), which supplies the shift of the substantive base of domination, the two technological consequences and the chapter's announcement of globalization as a new challenge.
CA search: "shift from military to economic power environmental spillover communication networks sovereignty 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's transitional argument. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson; the single dated anchor in this file is at Lesson 19.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Four sentences that change the subject

✅ The bridge from Part II to Part III is compressed into four sentences, and all four are examinable:

> However, the substantive base of domination in the contemporary world has been shifting from military power to economic power, spread over various centres. Besides, the use of superior technology has led to the spreading of environmental pollution all over the world, and linking of all parts of the world by an effective communication network. In short, the process of 'globalization' has emerged as a new challenge to the sovereignty of the nation-state.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.9, PDF p.214 (printed 197)

⚠️ Separate the three distinct claims, because they are not variations of one another:

```text
  CLAIM 1  THE BASE OF DOMINATION SHIFTS
             from MILITARY power -> to ECONOMIC power
             and it is "SPREAD OVER VARIOUS CENTRES"
                 -> not merely a change of currency; also a change
                    of STRUCTURE, from one centre to several

  CLAIM 2  SUPERIOR TECHNOLOGY produces TWO border-crossing effects
             (a) "spreading of ENVIRONMENTAL POLLUTION all over the
                  world"
             (b) "LINKING of all parts of the world by an EFFECTIVE
                  COMMUNICATION NETWORK"
                 -> one is a HARM that crosses borders
                 -> the other is a CAPACITY that crosses borders

  CLAIM 3  THEREFORE globalization "has emerged as a NEW CHALLENGE to
           the sovereignty of the nation-state"
```

🔑 Claim 1's trailing phrase, **spread over various centres**, is the most consequential three words in the transition and is absent from both canonical owners. ⚠️ Under bipolarity there were two centres, and a state could be constrained by one of them. ⚠️ If economic power is spread over **various** centres, then constraint no longer has an address. ⚠️ That is the structural difference between Part II and Part III, and it is what makes the third challenge harder to resist than the second: there is no camp to refuse to join.

### 2. Why pollution and communication are listed together

⚠️ At first sight these are an odd pair — one is an environmental harm, the other a technical capability. ⚠️ The chapter joins them because they share the property that matters for sovereignty: **neither can be stopped at a frontier**.

| ⚠️ | **Environmental pollution** | **Communication network** |
|---|---|---|
| What crosses | A harm | Information |
| Consent of the receiving state | ⚠️ Not sought | ⚠️ Not required |
| Can a border stop it? | ❌ No | ❌ No |
| Later evidence in the chapter | ✅ Chernobyl 1986; Indonesian forest fires; the ozone hole (L23) | ✅ Live telecast; famine coverage; electronic banking (L22-L23) |
| Effect on sovereignty | 🔑 The state cannot protect its territory | 🔑 The state cannot control what reaches its population |

⚠️ Together they destroy what Lesson 2 called **insulation**. 🔑 And insulation is the last of the four defences: self-rule returned at independence, economic self-determination was never returned, self-defence was lost to the blocs, and now the ability to hold the outside outside is lost too.

### 3. "In short" — the chapter's own summary verb

⚠️ The chapter introduces globalization with **in short**, which signals that the term is being used to *summarise* what has just been described rather than to introduce something new.

⚠️ This is worth noticing because it determines what globalization means in this chapter. ⚠️ It is not primarily a policy, an ideology or an institution; at this point in the text it is the name for a condition composed of: economic power spread over various centres, plus a border-crossing harm, plus a border-crossing communication capability. ⚠️ The policy sense — globalization as a deliberate programme — is introduced only later, at Lesson 25, and the chapter keeps the two apart.

```text
   GLOBALIZATION, sense 1 (here, p.214)  -- a CONDITION that has
     emerged: dispersed economic power + spillover + connection
                        |
   GLOBALIZATION, sense 2 (at p.217)     -- a POLICY adopted
     consciously: "a logical conclusion of the policy of
     liberalization and privatization"
                        |
   The chapter uses BOTH and never conflates them.
   An answer that conflates them cannot explain how a condition
   could be "adopted".
```

### 4. The transition as an argument about resistance

⚠️ Set the three challenges against the kind of resistance each permits, using only what the chapter has already established:

| ⚠️ Challenge | ⚠️ Where it comes from | ⚠️ Resistance available on the chapter's own account |
|---|---|---|
| Imperial rule | An identified foreign power | ✅ **Struggle for independence**, conducted **individually** |
| Neo-colonial dependence | **Forces**, operating through prices, capital, corporations | ⚠️ Collective producer organisation — a **semi-cartel** — available for one commodity and not others |
| Bloc pressure | Two identified superpowers and their camps | ✅ **Non-alignment** — refusal to be enlisted, at the cost of **criticism, pressures and threats** |
| Globalization | ⚠️ Economic power **spread over various centres**; pollution; networks | ⚠️ **No counterpart named at this point in the chapter** |

🔑 The empty cell is the point of the transition. ⚠️ Each earlier challenge had an addressee and therefore a possible strategy; this one, as introduced, has neither. ⚠️ Part III will eventually supply a response, but it is not resistance: it is the verdict at Lesson 30 that stopping the process **would be futile** and that the task is to **maximize its benefits** and **prevent its damaging effects**.

### 5. Answer use

- ⚠️ This is the paragraph that converts a two-part answer into a three-part one; without it, bloc politics and globalization look like separate subjects.
- ⚠️ In a stem that asks what replaced the Cold War constraint, the whole answer can be built on **shifting from military power to economic power, spread over various centres**.
- ❌ Do not write that globalization *began* here; ✅ the chapter says it **has emerged as a new challenge**, having been produced by the shift just described.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **The substantive base of domination in the contemporary world has been shifting from military power to economic power, spread over various centres.** (PDF p.214)
2. 🔑 **Spread over various centres** — constraint loses its address; there is no camp to refuse.
3. ✅ **The use of superior technology has led to the spreading of environmental pollution all over the world.**
4. ✅ And to **linking of all parts of the world by an effective communication network**.
5. ⚠️ The pair is joined by one shared property: neither can be stopped at a frontier.
6. 🔑 Together they remove **insulation**, the last of the four defences.
7. ✅ **In short, the process of 'globalization' has emerged as a new challenge to the sovereignty of the nation-state.**
8. ⚠️ **In short** marks globalization here as a *summary of a condition*, not yet a policy.
9. ⚠️ Two senses are used in the chapter and never conflated: the condition at p.214, the policy at p.217.
10. ⚠️ Each earlier challenge had an addressee and a strategy; this one, as introduced, has neither.
11. ⚠️ The chapter's eventual response is not resistance but management — **maximize its benefits**, **prevent its damaging effects**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain the significance of the phrase "spread over various centres", and show why it makes globalization a different kind of challenge from bloc pressure.

**Model answer:** ✅ The chapter says that **the substantive base of domination in the contemporary world has been shifting from military power to economic power, spread over various centres**. ⚠️ The first half changes the currency of domination; the trailing phrase changes its structure. ⚠️ Under bipolarity, constraint had an address: two camps, two leaders, two identifiable sources of **criticism, pressures and threats**, which is precisely what made **non-alignment** possible as a strategy — one can refuse to be enlisted only where there is somebody doing the enlisting. 🔑 If economic power is spread over various centres, there is no single party to refuse, negotiate with or balance against. ⚠️ The two technological effects reinforce this: pollution crosses borders without consent and the **effective communication network** links all parts of the world without requiring permission, so the state can neither protect its territory nor control what reaches its population. ⚠️ Hence the chapter introduces globalization with no counterpart to non-alignment, and ends Part III not with a strategy of resistance but with a programme of management.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat the shift from military to economic power as a reduction in the severity of the challenge. ⚠️ On the chapter's own structure it is an increase: the earlier constraints could be identified and resisted, and this one is introduced with no addressee at all.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** How does Gauba move from the end of the Cold War to the problem of globalization? *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ In three steps, all on one page. ✅ First, a structural claim: **the substantive base of domination in the contemporary world has been shifting from military power to economic power, spread over various centres** — the currency of domination changes, and so does its structure, from two centres to several. ✅ Second, two technological consequences: **the use of superior technology has led to the spreading of environmental pollution all over the world, and linking of all parts of the world by an effective communication network**. ⚠️ Both share the property that decides the chapter's argument — neither stops at a frontier — so together they end the state's capacity to insulate its territory and its population. ✅ Third, the conclusion: **in short, the process of 'globalization' has emerged as a new challenge to the sovereignty of the nation-state**. 🔑 Domination persists, loses its address, and becomes unresistable in the old way.

**Rubric [10]:** the shift of the substantive base **2** · the **spread over various centres** phrase with its structural significance **2** · both technological consequences **2** · the shared property that neither stops at a frontier **2** · the concluding sentence and the inference that constraint has lost its addressee **2**.

## Lesson 21 — Why economic issues took over international politics

Progress: 21 / 32 | Stage: Core | Block D — the process of globalization

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §4's third spine step on economic interdependence, and §5's distinction between bloc pressure and wider interdependence; with the Advanced companion §3's fifth step on the locus of power moving from military blocs to economic interdependence, read against PDF p.214 (printed 197), which supplies the global-problems premise, the periodisation of military primacy and the stated turn in the late 1970s and 1980s.
CA search: "economic issues international politics primacy energy dependence periodisation 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's periodisation. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The premise, and the narrowing it performs

✅ Part III opens with a general claim and then narrows it sharply:

> The emerging problems of global concern and magnitude call for global solution. These problems may touch many areas but primarily they are centred around economic issues.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.9, PDF p.214 (printed 197)

⚠️ Two moves in two sentences:

```text
  MOVE 1  SCALE ARGUMENT
          problems of GLOBAL concern and magnitude
              -> therefore they "call for GLOBAL SOLUTION"
              -> a solution at the level of one state is, by the
                 definition of the problem, inadequate
                      |
  MOVE 2  NARROWING
          "may touch many areas BUT PRIMARILY they are centred
           around ECONOMIC ISSUES"
              -> the chapter commits to an economic core, while
                 reserving the other areas
```

🔑 Move 1 is the sovereignty argument in its purest form and is easy to under-use. ⚠️ A problem that requires a global solution cannot be solved by a sovereign state acting alone, however complete its authority. ⚠️ So the inadequacy is not a failure of the state's power but a mismatch between the scale of the problem and the scale of the unit. ⚠️ That is a structural point, and it is why the chapter will end by asking for the *concept* to be revised rather than for states to try harder.

⚠️ Move 2 is a self-imposed limit worth respecting in answers: the chapter says **primarily**, which concedes that the other areas — environmental, communicative, cultural — are real and are not reducible to economics. ⚠️ Lesson 23 shows it keeping that promise.

### 2. The periodisation, which is unusually precise

✅ The chapter dates the change:

> For many years, the superpowers and their allies focused on the predominance of military power, and paid only secondary importance to the economic issues. For most of the 1950s, 1960s and early 1970s, lethal weapons in possession of a nation were regarded the symbols of its power; its economic assets and liabilities were not regarded so significant. However, in late 1970s and 1980s economic issues began to acquire greater importance in international politics.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.9, PDF p.214 (printed 197)

| ⚠️ Period | ✅ What counted as power | ✅ What did not |
|---|---|---|
| **Most of the 1950s, 1960s and early 1970s** | **Lethal weapons in possession of a nation were regarded the symbols of its power** | **Its economic assets and liabilities were not regarded so significant** |
| **Late 1970s and 1980s** | ✅ **Economic issues began to acquire greater importance in international politics** | ⚠️ Military power not said to cease mattering |

🔑 Note the chapter's careful verbs. ⚠️ Weapons were **regarded** the symbols of power; economic assets were **not regarded** so significant. ⚠️ Both are claims about what was *treated* as important, not about what *was* important. ⚠️ So the change the chapter describes is in the first instance a change of perception among states — and the evidence at Lesson 22 is then offered as the reason the perception changed.

⚠️ Note also what this does to the Part II chronology. ⚠️ The turn to economic issues is dated to the **late 1970s and 1980s**, which is *before* the 1991 collapse. 🔑 So the economic base of domination was already displacing the military one while the blocs still stood; the dissolution of one bloc did not cause the shift, it merely removed what had been obscuring it.

### 3. Why "symbols" is the right word

⚠️ The chapter says lethal weapons were **regarded the symbols of its power**, and the noun is exact. ⚠️ A symbol stands for something; the weapons stood for a state's standing in the world.

⚠️ This matters for the argument because symbols can be displaced without anything material changing. ⚠️ When economic assets and liabilities become the measure, a state with a large arsenal and large deficits is reclassified downward without losing a single weapon — which is precisely the observation the chapter makes at Lesson 22 about a state moving from **the world's greatest creditor nation** to **the world's greatest debtor nation**.

🔑 So the shift in the base of domination is in part a shift in what counts as a credential. ⚠️ And credentials are a sovereignty matter, because a state's standing in the international environment determines the terms on which it can act there.

### 4. Criticism, reply, residual

⚠️ **Criticism.** ⚠️ The periodisation is asserted and no mechanism is given for why perception changed when it did. ⚠️ Moreover, the implied direction is questionable: during the very period in which economic issues are said to have acquired greater importance, military expenditure and alliance competition continued, and the chapter itself records that one alliance remains **still going strong**.

⚠️ **Reply.** ⚠️ The chapter does not claim that military power ceased to matter; its claim is comparative — economic issues **began to acquire greater importance** — and it supplies the reason in the next paragraph, namely the discovery that **the economies of major powers were linked with the world economy**. ⚠️ On that account the change in perception follows a change in fact: once dependence on foreign energy, foreign capital and foreign markets became visible, economic position became a measure of vulnerability and therefore of power.

⚠️ **Residual.** ⚠️ The chapter still owes an account of why the dependence became visible when it did, and it does not supply one. ⚠️ Its evidence is a list of circumstances rather than a cause; an answer should present the periodisation as the chapter's framing, supported by illustration, and not as a demonstrated finding.

### 5. In the answer

⚠️ Use the periodisation as a dating device rather than as content. ⚠️ One sentence - that weapons were **regarded the symbols of its power** until **late 1970s and 1980s**, when **economic issues began to acquire greater importance** - establishes that the globalization argument is not a post-Cold-War afterthought, and that single point will separate a structural answer from a chronological one. ⚠️ Keep the chapter's **primarily** qualifier, because the environmental and cultural material later in the division depends on it.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **The emerging problems of global concern and magnitude call for global solution.** (PDF p.214)
2. 🔑 A problem requiring a global solution cannot be met by a single sovereign unit, however complete its authority — a mismatch of scale, not a failure of power.
3. ✅ **These problems may touch many areas but primarily they are centred around economic issues** — ⚠️ keep **primarily**; the chapter does not reduce everything to economics.
4. ✅ For **most of the 1950s, 1960s and early 1970s**, **lethal weapons in possession of a nation were regarded the symbols of its power**.
5. ✅ **Its economic assets and liabilities were not regarded so significant.**
6. ✅ **However, in late 1970s and 1980s economic issues began to acquire greater importance in international politics.**
7. ⚠️ The chapter's verbs — **regarded**, **not regarded** — make this first a change in what was treated as important.
8. 🔑 The turn is dated **before** 1991; the collapse of one bloc did not cause the shift, it uncovered it.
9. ⚠️ **Symbols** is exact: credentials can be displaced without anything material changing.
10. ⚠️ Objection: periodisation asserted without mechanism, and military competition continued; ⚠️ reply: the claim is comparative and is grounded in newly visible dependence; ⚠️ residual: no account of why it became visible when it did.

### Concept check

**Question:** The chapter dates the rise of economic issues to the late 1970s and 1980s. Why is that dating significant for its overall argument about power blocs and globalization?

**Model answer:** ⚠️ Because it falls before the dissolution of the bipolar order. ✅ The chapter places the turn in the **late 1970s and 1980s**, while the **dissolution of the one power bloc led by the former USSR** and the **consequent end of the 'cold war'** belong to **1991**. 🔑 So the economic base of domination was already displacing the military one while both blocs still stood. ⚠️ That protects the chapter from a serious objection: if the shift had been dated to 1991, globalization would look like a consequence of the Cold War's end, and an answer could argue that the constraint on sovereignty was simply a hangover from bipolarity. ⚠️ Dated earlier, the shift is independent of the blocs, and the collapse merely removed what had obscured it — which is consistent with the chapter's own formulation that the base **has been shifting**, a continuous tense covering both periods. ⚠️ It also makes **globalization** a genuinely third challenge rather than an epilogue to the second.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not write that the chapter claims military power ceased to matter. ⚠️ Its claim is comparative — economic issues **began to acquire greater importance** — and it records in the same breath that one military alliance was **still going strong**.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Why, according to Gauba, did economic issues acquire greater importance in international politics? *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ Because the problems that had become urgent were of a kind no single state could address. ✅ **The emerging problems of global concern and magnitude call for global solution**, and these problems, while touching many areas, **primarily... are centred around economic issues**. ✅ Previously the priorities ran the other way: for **most of the 1950s, 1960s and early 1970s**, **lethal weapons in possession of a nation were regarded the symbols of its power**, while **its economic assets and liabilities were not regarded so significant**. ✅ **However, in late 1970s and 1980s economic issues began to acquire greater importance in international politics**, as it was realised that **the economies of major powers were linked with the world economy**. ⚠️ The change is first of perception: once dependence became visible, economic position became the credential of standing. 🔑 Dated before 1991, the shift is independent of the Cold War's end.

**Rubric [10]:** the global-problems premise with the global-solution inference **2** · the **primarily economic** narrowing preserved **1** · the earlier periodisation with the symbols formulation **3** · the late-1970s-and-1980s turn **2** · the observation that the dating precedes 1991 **2**.

## Lesson 22 — Eight signs of a single world economy

Progress: 22 / 32 | Stage: Core | Block D — the process of globalization

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §4's statement that economic interdependence makes absolute external sovereignty harder to sustain; with the Advanced companion §8's comparative matrix row for globalization, which names electronic banking and oil shocks as book-period illustrations, read against PDF p.214 (printed 197), which supplies the full list of indicators that the economies of major powers had become linked with the world economy.
CA search: "world economy interdependence indicators energy dependence debtor creditor electronic banking 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's list of indicators. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The list, restored in full

⚠️ Neither canonical owner reproduces this list, and it is the chapter's principal body of evidence for the claim that one world economy exists. ✅ The passage:

> Now it was increasingly realized that the economies of major powers were linked with the world economy. For instance, it was discovered that the United States, Western Europe and Japan had become dependent on foreign sources of energy and other non-fuel mineral resources. Then, Japan had emerged as a major international economic actor whereas the United States was reduced from the world's greatest creditor nation to the world's greatest debtor nation. The Soviet Union and Eastern European states conceded that they too had immense budget deficits. Western Europe decided to create a single European Common Market. Third world countries were groaning under a heavy debt burden, and its large segments were still wanting in economic development. The centre of international economic growth had shifted to the western Pacific. And a large part of the world had been transformed into a single financial market thanks to the development of electronic banking.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.9, PDF p.214 (printed 197)

| # | ✅ Indicator | ⚠️ What kind of claim it is |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | **The United States, Western Europe and Japan had become dependent on foreign sources of energy and other non-fuel mineral resources** | ⚠️ Dependence of the strongest economies on inputs they do not control |
| 2 | **Japan had emerged as a major international economic actor** | ⚠️ Redistribution of economic standing |
| 3 | **The United States was reduced from the world's greatest creditor nation to the world's greatest debtor nation** | ⚠️ Reversal of position within one state's lifetime |
| 4 | **The Soviet Union and Eastern European states conceded that they too had immense budget deficits** | ⚠️ The condition crosses the ideological divide — **they too** |
| 5 | **Western Europe decided to create a single European Common Market** | ⚠️ A deliberate institutional response, not a symptom |
| 6 | **Third world countries were groaning under a heavy debt burden, and its large segments were still wanting in economic development** | ⚠️ The distributive dimension |
| 7 | **The centre of international economic growth had shifted to the western Pacific** | ⚠️ Geographical relocation of dynamism |
| 8 | **A large part of the world had been transformed into a single financial market thanks to the development of electronic banking** | 🔑 A technological cause producing a structural result |

### 2. Reading the list as an argument rather than a list

⚠️ The eight items are not eight examples of one thing. ⚠️ They work together, and the way they work is the examinable point:

```text
   1, 3, 4   THE STRONG ARE NOT EXEMPT
             energy dependence of the three largest economies;
             the largest creditor becomes the largest debtor;
             and "THEY TOO" -- the communist camp concedes deficits
                        |
   2, 7      POSITIONS ARE NOT FIXED
             Japan rises; the centre of growth moves to the
             western Pacific
                        |
   6         THE WEAK BEAR THE COST
             debt burden; segments "still wanting in economic
             development"
                        |
   8         THE STRUCTURE ITSELF HAS CHANGED
             "a SINGLE FINANCIAL MARKET thanks to ... ELECTRONIC
              BANKING"
                        |
   5         AND STATES HAVE ALREADY BEGUN TO RESPOND
             a "single European Common Market"
                        |
   CONCLUSION: interdependence is UNIVERSAL (not a developing-world
   condition), MOBILE (positions change), UNEQUAL (costs fall
   unevenly), STRUCTURAL (one financial market), and ALREADY
   ACKNOWLEDGED (institutional response under way)
```

🔑 Item 4 deserves particular attention. ⚠️ The words **they too** place the communist camp inside the same condition as the capitalist one. ⚠️ For a chapter that has just spent three pages on ideological blocs, that is a significant concession: the economic condition it is describing is not a feature of one system but of the world.

🔑 Item 5 is structurally different from the other seven. ⚠️ Seven are descriptions of a condition; this one is a **decision**. ⚠️ Its presence in the list quietly introduces the theme that Lesson 27 will develop into the five-rung ladder of regional economic integration: when interdependence is recognised, states respond by organising it rather than by resisting it.

### 3. The sovereignty implication, drawn carefully

⚠️ What exactly does this list prove about sovereignty? ⚠️ Less than it may seem, and it is important to be precise.

| ⚠️ Inference | ✅ or ❌ |
|---|---|
| States have lost legal independence | ❌ Nothing in the list supports this |
| States' economic outcomes depend substantially on decisions taken elsewhere | ✅ Items 1, 3, 6, 8 |
| Even the most powerful states are in this position | ✅ Items 1, 3, 4 |
| A state can restore its position by effort alone | ❌ Item 7 shows the centre of growth relocating, which no single policy commands |
| States are responding institutionally rather than by withdrawal | ✅ Item 5 |

🔑 The honest formulation is that the list establishes *mutual vulnerability*, not subordination. ⚠️ And mutual vulnerability is a different and in some ways more interesting challenge to the classical idea of external sovereignty, because it cannot be described as domination by anybody. ⚠️ That is precisely the condition the five questions at Lesson 24 are framed to address.

### 4. Handling these items in an answer

⚠️ Every one of the eight is a **2009**-edition statement about a period the chapter treats as recent. ❌ None may be used in the present tense. ✅ The safe construction names the chapter and the condition rather than the current fact:

```text
   SAFE    "Gauba's evidence that one world economy had come into
           being includes the dependence of the United States,
           Western Europe and Japan on foreign energy sources, and
           the transformation of a large part of the world into 'a
           single financial market thanks to the development of
           electronic banking'."
                        |
   UNSAFE  Any sentence asserting a present creditor, debtor,
           growth-centre or deficit position for a named state.
```

⚠️ Used correctly, items 1, 3, 4 and 8 are the highest-value quotations in Part III, because each is counter-intuitive: the strongest economies are dependent, the greatest creditor became the greatest debtor, the rival camp conceded the same difficulty, and the structural change was produced by a banking technology.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **It was increasingly realized that the economies of major powers were linked with the world economy.** (PDF p.214)
2. ✅ **The United States, Western Europe and Japan had become dependent on foreign sources of energy and other non-fuel mineral resources.**
3. ✅ **Japan had emerged as a major international economic actor.**
4. 🔑 **The United States was reduced from the world's greatest creditor nation to the world's greatest debtor nation.**
5. ✅ **The Soviet Union and Eastern European states conceded that they too had immense budget deficits** — ⚠️ **they too** crosses the ideological divide.
6. ✅ **Western Europe decided to create a single European Common Market** — ⚠️ the only item that is a decision rather than a condition.
7. ✅ **Third world countries were groaning under a heavy debt burden**, with **large segments... still wanting in economic development**.
8. ✅ **The centre of international economic growth had shifted to the western Pacific.**
9. 🔑 **A large part of the world had been transformed into a single financial market thanks to the development of electronic banking.**
10. ⚠️ Read together: interdependence is universal, mobile, unequal, structural and already being institutionally acknowledged.
11. 🔑 What the list proves is **mutual vulnerability**, not subordination — and that is the harder case for classical external sovereignty.
12. ❌ All eight are **2009**-edition statements; ❌ none may be used in the present tense.

### Concept check

**Question:** The chapter's eight indicators include one that is a decision rather than a condition, and one that reports the communist camp conceding the same difficulty as the capitalist camp. Identify both, and explain what each contributes that the other indicators do not.

**Model answer:** ✅ The decision is that **Western Europe decided to create a single European Common Market**. ⚠️ Every other indicator describes something that had happened to states; this one describes something states chose to do. ⚠️ Its contribution is to show that the response to recognised interdependence was institutional organisation rather than withdrawal — which is the theme the chapter develops later into **regional economic integration** as a process that **gives an impetus to globalization**. ✅ The cross-camp concession is that **the Soviet Union and Eastern European states conceded that they too had immense budget deficits**. 🔑 Its contribution is the phrase **they too**: in a chapter that has just devoted a whole division to two opposed ideological camps, this places both camps inside the same economic condition. ⚠️ That blocks an obvious objection — that the interdependence being described is a feature of capitalist economies — and supports the chapter's broader claim that the problem is one of scale rather than of system.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not use any of these eight items as a statement about the present. ⚠️ Creditor and debtor positions, growth centres, deficits and market arrangements are all **2009**-edition reports; the chapter's durable content is the structural claim that one world economy exists, not the standing of any named state within it.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** What evidence does Gauba offer that the economies of major powers had become linked with the world economy? *(Enumerate and explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ Eight items. ✅ **The United States, Western Europe and Japan had become dependent on foreign sources of energy and other non-fuel mineral resources**; **the United States was reduced from the world's greatest creditor nation to the world's greatest debtor nation**; **the Soviet Union and Eastern European states conceded that they too had immense budget deficits**; **third world countries were groaning under a heavy debt burden**; and **a large part of the world had been transformed into a single financial market thanks to the development of electronic banking**. ✅ One is a decision, not a condition: **Western Europe decided to create a single European Common Market**. ⚠️ Together: interdependence is universal, since the strongest are included; mobile, since positions reverse; unequal, since debt falls on the weakest; structural, since a banking technology made one market. 🔑 The finding is mutual vulnerability, not domination.

**Rubric [10]:** at least six indicators correctly reported **4** · the **they too** cross-camp point **1** · the Common Market identified as a decision **1** · the electronic-banking structural claim **1** · the four-way characterisation of the evidence **2** · the mutual-vulnerability conclusion **1**.

## Lesson 23 — Interdependence you can actually trace

Progress: 23 / 32 | Stage: Core | Block D — the process of globalization

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §7's list of the chapter's interdependence illustrations, with its caution that the exact page of the 2001 illustration had not been independently re-verified; with the Advanced companion §7's third internal debate, that borderless harms defeat the sealed-container view of external sovereignty, read against PDF pp.214-216 (printed 197-198), whose text runs continuously across the unnumbered overflow page PDF p.215.
CA search: "transnational spillover financial contagion environmental accident media transmission sovereignty 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's illustrative chains. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson; the single dated anchor in this file is at Lesson 19.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The oil case, and the fear that travelled

✅ The chapter's first illustration establishes the pattern:

> Increasing importance of economic issues in world politics became evident in so many other areas also. Thus, when Iraq conquered Kuwait in 1990, many countries feared that their economies would be hard hit by Iraq's control of a large part of the world's oil reserves.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.9, PDF p.214 (printed 197)

⚠️ Note the precise object of the claim. ⚠️ The chapter does not say that other economies were hit; it says that **many countries feared that their economies would be hard hit**. 🔑 What crossed the border instantaneously was an expectation. ⚠️ For a sovereignty argument that is a stronger observation than a material one: a state's economic prospects were altered by an event in a territory over which it had no authority, through a mechanism — anticipation — that no border control can intercept.

### 2. The chain that runs across three PDF pages

✅ The chapter then offers a worked chain, and it is the single most effective teaching device in Part III:

> Again the economies of various nations were so interlinked that they seemed to comprise a world economy. This may be illustrated by some examples. An aircraft built in America flew Japanese businessmen to Indonesia to secure oil for Japanese industries. French financiers used the same aircraft to track their investments in stock markets in London, New York, Tokyo and Hong Kong. When American consumers purchased less expensive East Asian textile products, U.S. textile manufacturers were forced to lay off workers. Then in order to strengthen their position in the textile market, American entrepreneurs sought to invest in high-technology West German textile machines. These machines were purchased with the money that was deposited by Saudi princes in Italian banks.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.9, PDF pp.214-216 (printed 197-198), running across the unnumbered overflow page PDF p.215

⚠️ The passage is exactly the one that crosses the chapter's typesetting overflow: PDF p.214 ends at **Tokyo**, the narrow column on PDF p.215 carries **and Hong Kong. When American consumers purchased less**, and PDF p.216 resumes at **expensive East Asian textile products**. ✅ Nothing is lost; the sentence is continuous.

```text
   ONE AIRCRAFT, THREE NATIONALITIES
     built in AMERICA | carries JAPANESE businessmen | to INDONESIA
     | to secure OIL for Japanese industry
                  |
     the SAME aircraft carries FRENCH financiers
     tracking investments in LONDON, NEW YORK, TOKYO and HONG KONG
                  |
   ONE PURCHASING DECISION, FOUR COUNTRIES
     AMERICAN consumers buy cheaper EAST ASIAN textiles
                  |
     U.S. textile manufacturers "were FORCED to LAY OFF WORKERS"
                  |
     American entrepreneurs invest in high-technology WEST GERMAN
     textile machines
                  |
     those machines bought with money DEPOSITED BY SAUDI PRINCES
     IN ITALIAN BANKS
                  |
   => a single consumer choice in one country reorganises employment
      in a second, capital goods demand in a third, and deploys
      savings originating in a fourth through banks in a fifth
```

🔑 The pedagogic value of the chain is that it contains no villain. ⚠️ Nobody in it is dominating anybody. ⚠️ A consumer buys a cheaper shirt; a manufacturer responds; a machine is bought; a deposit is lent. ⚠️ And a worker loses a job. ⚠️ This is the clearest possible demonstration of the difference between Part III and Parts I and II: here the constraint on a state's capacity to govern its own economy arises from ordinary transactions and not from anyone's strategy.

⚠️ Note also that the chapter's own wording marks the compulsion: manufacturers **were forced to lay off workers**. ⚠️ Force, with no one exercising it.

### 3. The second set: harms and images that cross borders

✅ The chapter then widens beyond economics, honouring the **primarily** qualifier of Lesson 21:

> Interlinkages between the economies of various nations may be illustrated further. When Japanese manufacturers lower the prices of various electronic gadgets, the prices of VCRs, TVs and microwave ovens fall in America. Nuclear accident at Chernobyl in the former USSR (1986) gave rise to the fear that its radiation had contaminated the food chain over half a world. When terrorists destroyed World Trade Centre in America (2001), whole world was shocked while watching its live telecast on TV. When famine sweeps Africa, TV viewers around the world are touched by the tragedy. Burning of forests in Indonesia brings climate change in whole South East Asia. News of a hole in the Earth's ozone layer over Antarctica comes as a warning of global disaster for the chemical industries world over.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.9, PDF p.216 (printed 198)

| # | ✅ Illustration | ⚠️ What crosses the border | ⚠️ Type |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Japanese manufacturers lower the prices of various electronic gadgets** → **prices of VCRs, TVs and microwave ovens fall in America** | A price | Economic |
| 2 | **Nuclear accident at Chernobyl in the former USSR (1986)** → **fear that its radiation had contaminated the food chain over half a world** | Radiation, and fear of it | Environmental |
| 3 | **Terrorists destroyed World Trade Centre in America (2001)** → **whole world was shocked while watching its live telecast on TV** | An image, in real time | Communicative |
| 4 | **Famine sweeps Africa** → **TV viewers around the world are touched by the tragedy** | Moral concern | Emotional |
| 5 | **Burning of forests in Indonesia brings climate change in whole South East Asia** | Climatic effect | Environmental, regional |
| 6 | **News of a hole in the Earth's ozone layer over Antarctica** → **a warning of global disaster for the chemical industries world over** | Scientific knowledge with economic consequence | Environmental-economic |

🔑 Together with the first set, these six complete the chapter's demonstration that what crosses a frontier can be a price, a radioactive particle, a television image, a feeling, a climatic change or a piece of scientific news. ⚠️ None of them is stopped by sovereignty, and none of them requires anyone's permission.

### 4. ⚠️ One owner uncertainty, resolved

⚠️ Both canonical owners carry an explicit caution about the 2001 illustration. 🔶 The Core owner records that the event is a valid dated illustration but that **its exact page placement in Gauba has not been independently re-verified here** and instructs the reader to **cite the event by date, not by a Gauba page**. 🔶 The Advanced companion repeats the caution and adds that **no specific Gauba page is asserted for it**.

✅ That uncertainty is resolved in this pass by direct extraction. ✅ The sentence **When terrorists destroyed World Trade Centre in America (2001), whole world was shocked while watching its live telecast on TV** appears on **PDF p.216 (printed 198)**, in the second interlinkage paragraph, between the Chernobyl sentence and the African famine sentence. ✅ The page may therefore be cited.

⚠️ The owners' substantive caution nonetheless stands and is adopted: the item is a **dated historical illustration**, ❌ never a current-affairs anchor, and its conceptual use — that a border-crossing event defeats the view of the state as a sealed container — is what the chapter is actually using it for.

### 5. The limit of illustration as evidence

⚠️ **Criticism.** ⚠️ Illustrations are not proof. ⚠️ A chain of six transactions assembled to show interconnection will show interconnection; it does not establish how much of a state's economic life is so determined, and it cannot distinguish deep interdependence from ordinary trade, which has existed for centuries.

⚠️ **Reply.** ⚠️ Two features of the chapter's examples do real work beyond illustration. ⚠️ First, *speed and automaticity*: the chapter's cases proceed without any decision by the affected state — manufacturers **were forced**, prices **fall**, viewers are **touched**, fear arises. ⚠️ Second, *the variety of what crosses*: the point is not that goods move but that radiation, images, concern and scientific warnings move on the same footing, so the border has ceased to be the relevant filter for any category of thing. ⚠️ Ordinary historical trade had neither property to the same degree.

⚠️ **Residual.** ⚠️ The chapter supplies no measure of magnitude and no threshold at which interdependence becomes a sovereignty problem rather than a fact about commerce. ⚠️ Its five questions at Lesson 24 are framed as questions precisely because it cannot answer that.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **When Iraq conquered Kuwait in 1990, many countries feared that their economies would be hard hit by Iraq's control of a large part of the world's oil reserves.** (PDF p.214)
2. 🔑 What crossed the border was an **expectation**, which no border control can intercept.
3. ✅ The aircraft chain: built in **America**, carrying **Japanese businessmen to Indonesia to secure oil**, then **French financiers** tracking investments in **London, New York, Tokyo and Hong Kong**.
4. ✅ The textile chain: cheaper **East Asian** products → U.S. manufacturers **forced to lay off workers** → investment in **high-technology West German textile machines** → bought with **money... deposited by Saudi princes in Italian banks**.
5. 🔑 The chain contains no dominating agent; the compulsion arises from ordinary transactions.
6. ✅ Six further illustrations: Japanese electronics and American prices; **Chernobyl... (1986)** and the **food chain over half a world**; the **World Trade Centre... (2001)** and its **live telecast**; **famine... Africa** and **TV viewers**; **burning of forests in Indonesia** and **climate change in whole South East Asia**; the **hole in the Earth's ozone layer over Antarctica** and the warning to **chemical industries world over**.
7. ⚠️ What crosses a frontier: a price, a particle, an image, a feeling, a climatic change, a scientific finding.
8. ✅ The owners' open question about the 2001 illustration's page is resolved: **PDF p.216 (printed 198)**.
9. ⚠️ The owners' substantive caution is retained: it is a dated historical illustration, ❌ not a current anchor.
10. ⚠️ The chain runs across the chapter's unnumbered overflow page, PDF p.215, with no loss of text.
11. ⚠️ Objection: illustration is not measurement; ⚠️ reply: speed, automaticity and the variety of what crosses do argumentative work; ⚠️ residual: no magnitude and no threshold is given.

### Concept check

**Question:** The chapter's textile chain contains no dominating agent. Explain why that absence makes it a more powerful illustration for Part III than any example from Parts I or II could be.

**Model answer:** ⚠️ Because Parts I and II both identified an agent, and an identified agent permits a strategy. ✅ Imperial rule was met by the **struggle for independence**; bloc pressure was met by **non-alignment**, the refusal to be enlisted. ⚠️ The textile chain has nobody to refuse. ✅ **American consumers purchased less expensive East Asian textile products**, so **U.S. textile manufacturers were forced to lay off workers**; those manufacturers then **sought to invest in high-technology West German textile machines**, which **were purchased with the money that was deposited by Saudi princes in Italian banks**. ⚠️ Every step is an ordinary transaction and nobody in the chain intends any effect on anyone's sovereignty. 🔑 Yet the chapter's own verb records compulsion — manufacturers **were forced** — so the outcome is coercive without a coercer. ⚠️ That is exactly the condition announced at the hand-off, where economic power is **spread over various centres**: a constraint with no address cannot be resisted by refusing an addressee, which is why Part III ends in management rather than in resistance.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat these as examples of foreign interference. ⚠️ No state in the chain acts against another, and the chapter's interdependence argument would be weakened, not strengthened, by reading intention into it: the force of the example lies in the fact that no one is responsible.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** "External sovereignty cannot operate as if states were sealed containers." Illustrate from Gauba's examples. *(Illustrate — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ The chapter's illustrations show several kinds of thing crossing frontiers without permission. ✅ A price: **Japanese manufacturers lower the prices of various electronic gadgets**, and **the prices of VCRs, TVs and microwave ovens fall in America**. ✅ A contaminant: the **nuclear accident at Chernobyl in the former USSR (1986)** raised the fear that **its radiation had contaminated the food chain over half a world**. ✅ An image: when **terrorists destroyed World Trade Centre in America (2001), whole world was shocked while watching its live telecast on TV**. ✅ A feeling: **when famine sweeps Africa, TV viewers around the world are touched by the tragedy**. ✅ A scientific warning: the **hole in the Earth's ozone layer over Antarctica** alarms **chemical industries world over**. ⚠️ Add an expectation — fear of **Iraq's control of a large part of the world's oil reserves** in **1990**. ⚠️ All are book-period illustrations. 🔑 No frontier filters any of these.

**Rubric [10]:** at least five illustrations correctly reported with their dates where given **5** · the identification of what crosses in each case **2** · the inference that the border has ceased to be a filter **2** · correct treatment of all items as book-period illustrations **1**.

## Lesson 24 — Five questions, and the demand they make

Progress: 24 / 32 | Stage: Core | Block D — the process of globalization

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §9's trap entry that the chapter presents challenge, constraint and revision rather than extinction; with the Advanced companion §7's statement that Gauba stops short of announcing the death of the state and instead requires external sovereignty to be revised suitably, read against PDF p.216 (printed 198), which supplies the five questions in sequence and the demand that follows them.
CA search: "duty of developed nations global commons responsibility for transnational harms 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's own framing of its questions. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The passage, and why it is the hinge of the chapter

✅ After the illustrations, the chapter stops describing and starts asking:

> Under the circumstances, it is natural to ask: if all nation-states have become interdependent in their economic relations, environmentally and even emotionally, how can a particular nation claim absolute sovereignty in its external relations? What is the duty of developed nations toward promoting economic growth in the developing societies? At what point the issues of national importance end and those of international importance begin? How can needy nations lay hands on those essential and rare resources which are the exclusive monopoly of the few nations? Which nations are responsible to control worldwide terrorism, drug-trafficking and environmental degradation? The concept of external sovereignty of nation-state will have to be revised suitably in order to deal with such situations.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.9, PDF p.216 (printed 198)

⚠️ Neither canonical owner reproduces the questions. ⚠️ They are the chapter's own statement of what the evidence has made unanswerable, and each one attacks a different attribute of classical external sovereignty.

### 2. The five questions, and what each one attacks

| # | ✅ Question | 🔑 The classical attribute it attacks |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | **If all nation-states have become interdependent in their economic relations, environmentally and even emotionally, how can a particular nation claim absolute sovereignty in its external relations?** | **Absoluteness** — the claim to be subject to no superior |
| 2 | **What is the duty of developed nations toward promoting economic growth in the developing societies?** | **Non-obligation** — that a sovereign owes no external duty it has not consented to |
| 3 | **At what point the issues of national importance end and those of international importance begin?** | **Domestic jurisdiction** — the existence of a reserved sphere |
| 4 | **How can needy nations lay hands on those essential and rare resources which are the exclusive monopoly of the few nations?** | **Exclusive territorial control** of resources within one's own borders |
| 5 | **Which nations are responsible to control worldwide terrorism, drug-trafficking and environmental degradation?** | **Territorial allocation of responsibility** — that each state polices its own territory and that this suffices |

🔑 Read down the right-hand column and the structure appears: the five questions take apart five different components of external sovereignty, one each. ⚠️ Taken singly, each is a difficulty; taken together, they are the chapter's case that the concept requires revision.

⚠️ Note the asymmetry inside the list. ⚠️ Questions 1 and 3 are conceptual — they ask what a term can mean. ⚠️ Questions 2 and 5 are normative — they ask who owes what and who answers for what. ⚠️ Question 4 is distributive — it asks how an entitlement held by a few can be reached by others. ⚠️ So the chapter is claiming that interdependence generates problems of three different kinds, which is why no single adjustment answers all of them.

### 3. The demand, and its exact modesty

✅ **The concept of external sovereignty of nation-state will have to be revised suitably in order to deal with such situations.**

⚠️ Weigh each element:

```text
   "THE CONCEPT ... of external sovereignty"
        -> the object of revision is the CONCEPT, not the
           institution, not the state, not the states-system

   "WILL HAVE TO BE"
        -> necessity, and prospective: not "has been", not "should
           be considered"

   "REVISED"
        -> not abolished, not replaced, not abandoned

   "SUITABLY"
        -> no content is specified; the chapter does not say HOW

   "IN ORDER TO DEAL WITH SUCH SITUATIONS"
        -> the criterion of a successful revision is PRACTICAL
           adequacy to the five problems, not theoretical elegance
```

🔑 This is the sentence the whole topic has been building towards, and its modesty is deliberate. ⚠️ It concedes everything that the critics of globalisation-scepticism want — the concept as it stands cannot handle these situations — while conceding nothing to the view that the state is finished. ⚠️ An answer that quotes it accurately has the chapter's position exactly; an answer that renders it as "sovereignty is obsolete" has substituted a different and unsupported claim.

⚠️ **Criticism.** ⚠️ **Suitably** is doing an enormous amount of unexamined work. ⚠️ A demand for revision without any specification of the revision is close to an admission that the chapter does not know what the answer is.

⚠️ **Reply.** ⚠️ Two defences are available from the chapter itself. ⚠️ First, the three perspectives at Lesson 31 are precisely three rival specifications of what the revision should be, and the chapter sets them out without adjudicating — which is a presentation of options, not an evasion. ⚠️ Second, the chapter's final verdict at Lesson 30 supplies a practical criterion: **maximize its benefits for humanity and prevent its damaging effects**. ⚠️ A revision is suitable if it enables that.

⚠️ **Residual.** ⚠️ Even granting both defences, the chapter never says whether the revised concept would still be *sovereignty*, or what would remain of absoluteness, exclusivity and reserved jurisdiction after the five questions were accommodated. ⚠️ That is the question Lesson 32 has to face, and the chapter leaves it open.

### 4. How to deploy the five questions

⚠️ They are the most efficient structure available for a 20-mark answer on this topic, because they are the chapter's own and they are exhaustive of its case.

```text
   OPENING   state the condition: interdependence "in their economic
             relations, environmentally and EVEN EMOTIONALLY"
                        |
   BODY      take the five questions as five sub-headings, each with
             one illustration from Lesson 23:
               Q1 absoluteness      <- the textile chain
               Q2 duty              <- third-world debt burden
               Q3 the line          <- Chernobyl and the food chain
               Q4 rare resources    <- oil reserves, 1990
               Q5 responsibility    <- ozone, forest fires, terrorism
                        |
   CLOSE     the demand, quoted exactly, with its five components
             (concept / necessity / revision / unspecified / practical)
```

⚠️ Note the chapter's own phrase **even emotionally** in the opening clause. ⚠️ It is easy to pass over and it is the chapter conceding that its account of interdependence is not confined to material flows — the famine illustration at Lesson 23 is what it rests on.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The questions arise because **all nation-states have become interdependent in their economic relations, environmentally and even emotionally**. (PDF p.216)
2. ✅ Q1: **how can a particular nation claim absolute sovereignty in its external relations?** — attacks **absoluteness**.
3. ✅ Q2: **what is the duty of developed nations toward promoting economic growth in the developing societies?** — attacks **non-obligation**.
4. ✅ Q3: **at what point the issues of national importance end and those of international importance begin?** — attacks **domestic jurisdiction**.
5. ✅ Q4: **how can needy nations lay hands on those essential and rare resources which are the exclusive monopoly of the few nations?** — attacks **exclusive control of resources**.
6. ✅ Q5: **which nations are responsible to control worldwide terrorism, drug-trafficking and environmental degradation?** — attacks **territorial allocation of responsibility**.
7. ⚠️ Three kinds of question: conceptual (1, 3), normative (2, 5), distributive (4).
8. 🔑 **The concept of external sovereignty of nation-state will have to be revised suitably in order to deal with such situations.**
9. ⚠️ Five components of that sentence: the **concept** is the object; **will have to be** is necessity; **revised** is not abolished; **suitably** is unspecified; the criterion is practical adequacy.
10. ⚠️ Objection: **suitably** is unexamined; ⚠️ reply: the three perspectives are three candidate specifications, and the closing verdict supplies a practical test; ⚠️ residual: the chapter never says what would remain of sovereignty afterwards.
11. ⚠️ **Even emotionally** concedes that interdependence is not confined to material flows.

### Concept check

**Question:** Each of the chapter's five questions attacks a different attribute of classical external sovereignty. Identify the attribute attacked by any three of them, and explain why the chapter's conclusion is "revised" rather than "abandoned".

**Model answer:** ✅ Question 1 — **how can a particular nation claim absolute sovereignty in its external relations?** — attacks **absoluteness**, the claim to be subject to no superior. ✅ Question 3 — **at what point the issues of national importance end and those of international importance begin?** — attacks the existence of a **reserved domestic sphere**, because interdependence removes the line that the reservation presupposes. ✅ Question 5 — **which nations are responsible to control worldwide terrorism, drug-trafficking and environmental degradation?** — attacks the **territorial allocation of responsibility**, since harms that originate in no one jurisdiction cannot be policed by the ordinary rule that each state answers for its own territory. ⚠️ The conclusion is **revised** because each question identifies an inadequacy of a component rather than the falsity of the concept: none of them shows that states have ceased to be independent entities in law, and question 2's demand for a **duty of developed nations** actually presupposes states as the bearers of obligations. 🔑 A concept whose components need adjustment is a concept to be revised; the chapter's own words are that it **will have to be revised suitably in order to deal with such situations**.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat the five questions as rhetorical questions with the answer "no sovereignty". ⚠️ Two of them — the duty of developed nations, and responsibility for transnational harms — are asking which states owe and answer, and they would be unintelligible if states had ceased to be the relevant units.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Set out the questions Gauba raises about external sovereignty and state the conclusion he draws. *(Enumerate and state — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ Because nation-states have become interdependent **in their economic relations, environmentally and even emotionally**, the chapter asks: **how can a particular nation claim absolute sovereignty in its external relations?**; **what is the duty of developed nations toward promoting economic growth in the developing societies?**; **at what point the issues of national importance end and those of international importance begin?**; **how can needy nations lay hands on those essential and rare resources which are the exclusive monopoly of the few nations?**; and **which nations are responsible to control worldwide terrorism, drug-trafficking and environmental degradation?** ⚠️ The five attack five attributes — absoluteness, freedom from obligation, reserved jurisdiction, exclusive control of resources, territorial allocation of responsibility. ✅ The conclusion is limited: **the concept of external sovereignty of nation-state will have to be revised suitably in order to deal with such situations**. 🔑 Revision, not abolition, on a practical criterion.

**Rubric [10]:** all five questions reported **5** · at least three attributes correctly identified **2** · the conclusion quoted accurately **2** · recognition that the conclusion is revision rather than abolition **1**.
