## Anchor 1 — 2020, `URC-B-PHLY`, Q4(b) [15] — ➡️ routed in from Topic 11

> ✅ "State and explain the relevance of the doctrine of sovereignty in times of globalization."

- ✅ **Ledger's primary owner:** the Philosophy sovereignty file — ➡️ **Topic 11**, which routed the part here.
- ⚠️ **Why it was routed:** Topic 11 records that its own chapters end at the point where Chapter 9 begins, and that this stem's substantive demand — what globalization does to the doctrine — lies **on the far side of that line**.
- ⚠️ **Directive:** *State and explain* — a two-part instruction: set out the doctrine, then account for its relevance under a named condition.
- ⚠️ **Conceptual demand:** the candidate must hold the doctrine fixed while the condition changes, and say what of it survives.
- ✅ **What this chapter supplies:** the entire external-challenge account — Lessons 20 to 24 for the condition; Lesson 24's five questions, each of which attacks a named attribute of the doctrine; Lesson 24's demand that the concept **be revised suitably**; Lesson 30's verdict; and Lesson 31's three perspectives, of which the realist entry states in terms that globalization **does not transcend state sovereignty**.
- ❌ **What this chapter does not supply:** the doctrine itself — *superanus*, Garner, the legal domain, the five characteristics, the monist line, popular sovereignty. ➡️ All of that is **Topic 11**, and the *state* half of the directive is answered from there.
- ❌ **No answer approach is offered here**, because the stem's primary owner is not this topic.

## Anchor 2 — 2018, `EGT-D-PHLY`, Q3(a) [20]

> ✅ "What do you understand by multi-culturalism ? How are globalization and multi-culturalism related ? How does their relationship affect cultural changes ?"

- ✅ **Ledger's primary owner:** the Philosophy humanism, secularism and multiculturalism file.
- ⚠️ **Directive:** three linked questions — define, relate, then assess an effect.
- ⚠️ **Conceptual demand:** the second and third questions require an account of what globalization does to culture, in both directions.
- ✅ **What this chapter supplies:** Lesson 25's consequence that globalization **distributes standard goods and services to every nook and cranny of the world and thus introduces uniform style of living and uniform ways of entertainment**, and **contributes to the development of a global culture which is expected to create greater understanding among different peoples of the world**; Lesson 29's rival charge that **Western culture is being projected as global culture through the so-called globalization, and thereby the cultural heritage of the East is being eroded**; Lesson 12's Nkrumah channel of **a variety of educational and cultural institutions**; and Lesson 30's double-edged finding that globalization both **disintegrated communities** and **liberated** subordinate groups **from the ideological domination of their local communities**.
- ❌ **What this chapter does not supply:** any definition or theory of multiculturalism, its descriptive and normative dimensions, or its relation to secularism and pluralism. ➡️ Those belong to the owner file.
- ❌ **No answer approach is offered here.**

## Anchor 3 — 2022, `CRNA-S-PHLY`, Q3(a) [20]

> ✅ "Do you agree that economic development does not on its own lead to human development and social progress? Give reasons and justifications for your answer."

- ✅ **Ledger's primary owner:** the Philosophy development and social progress file.
- ⚠️ **Directive:** *Do you agree... give reasons and justifications* — a position is required, with grounds.
- ⚠️ **Conceptual demand:** evidence that growth and human betterment can come apart.
- ✅ **What this chapter supplies:** Lesson 30's balance passage in both directions — the concession that globalization **has failed to eradicate poverty from the world or reduce economic disparities** and has **caused environmental degradation, prompted intolerance, encouraged militarism, disintegrated communities and worsened the condition of subordinate groups**, set against the claims that it **raised real per capita income in the world to three times since 1945** and **reduced the ratio of the extremely poor in world population to a half**. ⚠️ That pairing is precisely a case of aggregate economic gain alongside unimproved or worsened human outcomes. ✅ Lesson 10's loop — low margins preventing the accumulation that alone could end low margins — supplies the structural half.
- ❌ **What this chapter does not supply:** any theory of human development, capability, welfare or social progress, and no measure of any of them. ➡️ Those belong to the owner file.
- ❌ **No answer approach is offered here.**

## Anchor 4 — 2019, `SDF-B-PHLY`, Q3(c) [15]

> ✅ "What do you consider to be the future of Marxism in the context of the prevalent free-market economy?"

- ✅ **Ledger's primary owner:** the Philosophy political ideologies file.
- ⚠️ **Directive:** *What do you consider* — a judgment, with grounds, about a doctrine's prospects under a stated condition.
- ⚠️ **Conceptual demand:** whether a Marxian analysis retains explanatory purchase in a liberalised global economy.
- ✅ **What this chapter supplies:** the durability of one Marxian analytical structure across a century, in the chapter's own text — Lenin's **economic necessity of capitalist economy** with its three driving forces (**1916**); Nkrumah's work, which the chapter says **follows the style and line of argument of Lenin's** (**1965**); and the radical perspective at Lesson 31, which calls globalization **nothing but the latest stage in the development of international capitalism** and **at best... an instrument of neo-colonialism**. ⚠️ The chapter also supplies the counter-material: Lesson 22's evidence that the strongest economies are themselves dependent, and Lesson 30's two quantified gains.
- ❌ **What this chapter does not supply:** historical materialism, alienation, class, the theory of the state, or any assessment of Marxism as a philosophical doctrine. ➡️ Those belong to the owner file and to **Topic 04**.
- ❌ **No answer approach is offered here.**

## Anchor 5 — 2025, `SLPM-B-PHL`, Q3(c) [15]

> ✅ "Is it possible to reconcile the concept of development with tribal values to bring social and economic progress? Discuss."

- ✅ **Ledger's primary owner:** the Philosophy development and social progress file.
- ⚠️ **Directive:** *Is it possible... discuss* — a reconciliation question, requiring both the tension and the conditions of its resolution.
- ⚠️ **Conceptual demand:** whether development as ordinarily pursued is compatible with the integrity of a community's own values.
- ✅ **What this chapter supplies:** Lesson 30's unresolved pair, which is exactly this tension stated at the level of the world — globalization **disintegrated communities**, and it **liberated** subordinate groups **from the ideological domination of their local communities and enabled them to fight for their legitimate rights**. ⚠️ The chapter thereby supplies the general form of the problem: the weakening of a community's hold is a loss to the community and a gain to those it subordinates, and the valuation depends on one's position within it. ✅ Lesson 25's **uniform style of living and uniform ways of entertainment** supplies the homogenising mechanism.
- ❌ **What this chapter does not supply:** any treatment of tribal societies, their values, rights or institutions, and no Indian policy material of any kind. ➡️ Those belong to the owner file and to the Indian Society and Social Justice owners.
- ❌ **No answer approach is offered here.**

## Parts examined and not carried

| ⚠️ Category | ✅ Treatment |
|---|---|
| Thirteen sovereignty-owned parts, 2018-2026 — Bodin, Austin, Laski, Kautilya, monism against pluralism | ➡️ **Topic 11**; ❌ not carried here, because this chapter teaches none of the doctrines they test |

| Every other Section-A part across the nine papers | ⚠️ Read and not relevant |

# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

⚠️ Six checks. ⚠️ Each requires material from at least three lessons, which is the skill an examiner tests whenever a stem spans the chapter's divisions. ⚠️ They are ordered so that the first two consolidate the mechanisms, the next two the arguments, and the last two the chapter as a whole.

---

## Check A — one distinction, six appearances

**Prompt:** The separation of legal title from effective capacity is introduced once and then used repeatedly. Identify six places where it does work, and say what it settles in each.

**Worked response:** ⚠️ **One — the chapter's own limb (b) (L1).** ✅ External challenges threatened **autonomy of the nation-state**, not its authority; the noun chooses the distinction before any evidence arrives. ⚠️ **Two — the neo-colonial definition (L9).** ✅ The strategy **does not maintain its political domination in a foreign territory, but continues its economic exploitation**; the negative limb concedes title and the positive limb removes capacity. ⚠️ **Three — Part I's verdict (L13).** ✅ A **'sovereign, democratic republic' may still feel constrained by the forces of neo-colonialism** — both propositions, of one country, at one time. ⚠️ **Four — the bloc argument (L17).** ✅ **No nation-state remained capable of providing for an effective security of its citizens by conventional means**; capacity fails while statehood does not. ⚠️ **Five — the five questions (L24).** ✅ Each attacks an attribute of the concept, not the existence of the state, which is why the conclusion is that the concept **will have to be revised suitably**. ⚠️ **Six — the realist entry (L31).** ✅ Globalization **does not transcend state sovereignty**; the title survives, which is the half of the distinction the chapter never withdraws. 🔑 Six uses, one distinction, and the chapter's entire argument lives in the gap it opens.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not use the distinction to say that nothing has happened. ⚠️ The chapter's demand for revision and its insistence that damaging effects be **prevented** show that it treats an unexercisable title as a real problem, not a verbal one.

---

## Check B — the chapter's only remedy, three applications

**Prompt:** Across its three divisions the chapter names one kind of response to lost autonomy. Identify it, locate three applications, and state the paradox it carries.

**Worked response:** ⚠️ The response is **collective organisation**, and the chapter never names another. ⚠️ **One — the producers' remedy (L10).** ✅ Prices are **largely dictated by the rich countries until they can organize themselves into a semi-cartel like the Organization of the Petroleum Exporting Countries (OPEC) (founded in 1960)**, and ❌ **for other products, such organizations simply do not exist**. ⚠️ **Two — the strategic remedy (L16).** ✅ Non-alignment, adopted under **Nehru (of India), Nasser (of Egypt) and Tito (of Yugoslavia)**, **evolved into a movement as well as a common forum for the new nations**, with aims including **cooperation between the developing countries** and organisations for **industry and trade, technological development, education and information**. ⚠️ **Three — the economic remedy (L27).** ✅ **Groups of certain countries seek to eliminate the artificial barriers in the way of international trade and competition**, through five named forms, and **this process gives an impetus to globalization**. ⚠️ A fourth application appears below the level of the state: subordinate groups were **inspired... to form their global organizations for their emancipation** (L30). 🔑 The paradox: every one of these recovers capacity by accepting a common discipline, so the instrument of recovery is also an instrument of constraint — and the chapter never comments on this.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not present collective organisation as the chapter's recommendation. ⚠️ It is what the chapter's evidence shows states and groups actually doing; the chapter's own recommendation is the much vaguer injunction to **devise suitable ways and means**.

---

## Check C — four mechanisms, four kinds of compulsion

**Prompt:** The chapter describes four mechanisms by which autonomy is reduced. For each, identify who, if anyone, is doing the compelling.

**Worked response:** ✅ **Imperial rule.** ⚠️ An identified foreign power, acting deliberately, **exploiting the natural and human resources of those countries to strengthen their own economies**; compulsion is direct and attributable. ✅ **Neo-colonial dependence.** ⚠️ Partly attributable and partly structural: prices are **largely dictated by the rich countries**, which names an agent, but the **vast size and population and low level of industrial development** that keep a country a supplier compel nobody and are compelled by nobody. ⚠️ **Brain drain** is the extreme case — professionals leave **for want of adequate career opportunities in their own countries**, so the mechanism runs through the free choices of the subordinate society's own members. ✅ **Bloc pressure.** ⚠️ Two identified superpowers; compulsion is attributable and is described in the language of command — **bowing to the commands of a superpower** — though states **felt constrained** rather than being ordered to join. ✅ **Globalization.** ⚠️ Nobody at all: economic power is **spread over various centres**, and in the chapter's textile chain a consumer's purchase leaves manufacturers **forced to lay off workers** with no one exercising force. 🔑 The series runs from a named compeller to no compeller, and resistance becomes correspondingly harder at each step.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not read intention into the globalization examples. ⚠️ Their force depends on the absence of an agent; supplying one would turn the chapter's strongest illustration into an ordinary complaint about foreign interference.

---

## Check D — three places where the chapter contradicts itself, and how to handle each

**Prompt:** The chapter prints incompatible statements in three places. Locate them and state the correct handling.

**Worked response:** ⚠️ **One — the lending institutions.** ✅ The box says the Fund **provides assistance on concessional terms to low-income countries to enable them to carry out such reforms** (L26); ✅ the critics' paragraph on the same page says globalization implies **acceptance of terms and conditions dictated by International Monetary Fund (IMF), World Bank and other Western-dominated agencies** (L29). ⚠️ Handling: report both registers, and state that the dispute is not about what happens but about whether the consent is real. ⚠️ **Two — global culture.** ✅ The chapter reports that globalization **contributes to the development of a global culture which is expected to create greater understanding among different peoples of the world** (L25); ✅ the critics reply that **Western culture is being projected as global culture... and thereby the cultural heritage of the East is being eroded** (L29). ⚠️ Handling: note that the first is an **expectation** reported and the second its denial, and that the chapter adjudicates neither. ⚠️ **Three — inevitability.** ✅ The critics say underdeveloped countries are **being entrapped by projecting the inevitability of globalization** (L29); ✅ the chapter itself writes **when globalization became inevitable** (L25) and concludes that stopping it **would be futile** (L30). ⚠️ Handling: distinguish *cannot now be stopped*, which the chapter's evidence supports, from *was bound to happen and should not be opposed*, which its six conceded harms and its instruction to **prevent its damaging effects** show it does not hold. 🔑 In all three, the examiner is rewarding the candidate who notices rather than the candidate who picks a side.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not treat any of these as an error to be corrected. ⚠️ The chapter's method throughout is to state a position in its proponents' terms and the objection in its critics' terms; the defect is only that it does not always mark which voice is speaking.

---

## Check E — the chapter's numbers, and what may be said about them

**Prompt:** The chapter supplies a small number of quantified claims. List them, state their exact form, and say what may and may not be done with each.

**Worked response:** ✅ **Two substantive quantities**, both from the balance passage (L30): globalization **has raised real per capita income in the world to three times since 1945**, and **has reduced the ratio of the extremely poor in world population to a half**. ❌ Neither may be converted to a percentage, re-dated, turned into a count of persons, attributed to any dataset or agency, or updated. ⚠️ Both are the balancing writers' claims as reported in a **2009** edition, and the first is dated from a base year three decades before the policy it is credited to. ✅ **Dated events used as illustrations:** **Russian Revolution (1917)**; **the Second World War (1939-45)**; India's independence, **1947**; **NATO**, **founded in 1949**; **OPEC (founded in 1960)**; **Warsaw Pact**, **founded in 1955**, with **Albania (which left it in 1968)**; Gorbachev's reforms, **1985**; **Chernobyl... (1986)**; **Iraq conquered Kuwait in 1990**; the collapse **by 1991**; the **World Trade Centre... (2001)**. ⚠️ All are historical or book-period illustrations; ❌ none may be used in the present tense. ✅ **Bibliographic dates**, which are not subject to that restriction: Hobson **1902**, Lenin **1916**, Said **1993**, Nkrumah **1965**, and the life dates the chapter gives for **Nkrumah (1909-72)**, **Lippman (1889-1974)** and **Sauvy (1898-1990)**. 🔑 Three categories, three different handling rules.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not treat the tripling claim as an argument that globalization reduced poverty. ⚠️ The same sentence concedes that globalization **failed to eradicate poverty from the world or reduce economic disparities**; a falling ratio of the extremely poor is compatible with both, and a careful answer says so.

---

## Check F — the whole topic in one question

**Prompt:** A stem asks simply: "What has happened to external sovereignty?" Build the answer's spine from the chapter, naming the evidence at each step, and close where the chapter closes.

**Worked response:** ⚠️ **Step one — fix the question.** ✅ The chapter divides challenges into internal, **brought out by the pluralist theory of sovereignty**, and external, **from international environment which threatened autonomy of the nation-state**, with globalization as a process that **further aggravated the situation**. ⚠️ **Step two — the first removal.** ✅ Empire took self-rule for the **expansion of their trade and industry after their own national consolidation**; decolonization returned it, but **the exploitation of the former colonies... did not stop with the achievement of their independence**. ⚠️ **Step three — the mechanisms that survived.** ✅ Prices **largely dictated by the rich countries**; **labour-intensive industries with a low margin of profit** against **capital-intensive and technology-intensive industries with a high margin of profit**; MNCs earning **huge profits for the countries of their origin**; components **assembled elsewhere**; and **'brain drain'**. ⚠️ **Step four — the second removal.** ✅ With superpower alliances, **nuclear weapons and Intercontinental Ballistic Missiles (ICBMs)**, **no nation-state remained capable of providing for an effective security of its citizens by conventional means**; non-alignment resisted at the cost of **criticism, pressures and threats from both side** and **came to stay**. ⚠️ **Step five — the third removal.** ✅ The **substantive base of domination... shifting from military power to economic power, spread over various centres**, with pollution and an **effective communication network** ending insulation, and interdependence **in their economic relations, environmentally and even emotionally**. ⚠️ **Step six — the verdict.** ✅ **The concept of external sovereignty of nation-state will have to be revised suitably**; stopping the process **would be futile**; **we must... maximize its benefits for humanity and prevent its damaging effects**. ⚠️ **Step seven — the stopping point.** ⚠️ **Suitably** is never specified, the managing **we** is never identified, and the chapter's last page prints, without correction, a realist view that globalization **does not transcend state sovereignty**. 🔑 Title intact, capacity reduced, concept unsettled.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not end on either extreme. ⚠️ The chapter rules out abolition by presupposing a capable state in both its remedial sentences, and rules out complacency by conceding six harms and demanding revision; an answer that lands on either pole has contradicted the source it is citing.

---
# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

⚠️ Six original questions — two at each weight — distinct from the thirty-two lesson-local questions above and from every stem in the application-anchor section. ⚠️ Ceilings observed: **10-mark models within 150 words; 15- and 20-mark models within 250 words.** ⚠️ Each rubric is quantified and unique.

---

## Question 1 [10 marks]

**Question:** "Decolonization transferred the instrument and left the object in place." Examine. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model:** ✅ The instrument was political control; the object was an economic arrangement. ✅ Colonies were sought **for the expansion of their trade and industry after their own national consolidation**, and the powers **started exploiting the natural and human resources of those countries to strengthen their own economies**. ✅ When **colonial rulers began to concede independence**, the instrument passed; ✅ but **the exploitation of the former colonies of Asia, Africa and Latin America did not stop with the achievement of their independence**, because the arrangement survived in **new, subtle means**. ✅ Prices stayed **largely dictated by the rich countries**; the absence of **capital and technical expertise** confined production to **low margin** industries; corporations returned **huge profits for the countries of their origin**. ⚠️ The verb **concede** is the hinge: what is conceded is chosen by the conceder. 🔑 Hence a **'sovereign, democratic republic' may still feel constrained**.

**Rubric [10]:** the instrument/object distinction stated **2** · the trade-and-industry purpose with the consolidation clause **2** · the did-not-stop finding **2** · at least two surviving mechanisms **2** · the significance of **concede** **1** · the closing verdict quoted **1**.

---

## Question 2 [10 marks]

**Question:** Why does Gauba treat the end of the Cold War as a change of instrument rather than a restoration of autonomy? *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model:** ✅ Because only one side of the structure ended. ✅ Gorbachev's **1985** reforms produced **relaxation of the Soviet Union's control over the communist nations of Eastern Europe** and the **eventual collapse of the communist system in most of these countries by 1991**, marking **the dissolution of the one power bloc led by the former USSR and consequent end of the 'cold war'**; the **Warsaw Pact also became redundant**. ✅ But **the North Atlantic Treaty Organization (NATO) is still going strong**, so **from military angle, only one power bloc is left now**. ✅ Decisively, the next sentence records that **the substantive base of domination in the contemporary world has been shifting from military power to economic power, spread over various centres**, with technology spreading pollution and linking the world by **an effective communication network**. ✅ Hence globalization **has emerged as a new challenge**. 🔑 Domination persists; only its base moves.

**Rubric [10]:** the reform-to-collapse sequence with both dates **3** · the asymmetry between the two alliances **2** · the **from military angle** qualifier **1** · the shift of the substantive base quoted **2** · the two technological consequences **1** · the inference stated as a conclusion **1**.

---

## Question 3 [15 marks]

**Question:** "Neo-colonialism is a structural condition, not a strategy." Critically examine with reference to Gauba's own account. *(Critically examine — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Model:** ✅ The chapter supports both readings and reconciles neither. ✅ Its definition is intentional: neo-colonialism **denotes the strategy of a colonial power which does not maintain its political domination in a foreign territory, but continues its economic exploitation by using it as a source of cheap labour and raw materials as well as a big market for its industrial products**. ✅ Nkrumah's charge is likewise agentive — **the ex-colonial powers and newly emerging superpowers such as the United States continued to play a decisive role through international monetary bodies, and through the fixing of prices on world markets, multinational corporations and cartels and a variety of educational and cultural institutions**.

✅ Yet the explanation offered immediately afterwards requires no intention. ✅ The new nations, **with their vast size and population and low level of industrial development, remain the chief source of supply of raw materials and labour-intensive products**; lacking **capital and technical expertise** they are **forced to make use of their resources in labour-intensive industries with a low margin of profit**, so that **international trade continuously operates to the disadvantage of the developing countries**. ⚠️ **Brain drain** is the clearest case: professionals leave **for want of adequate career opportunities in their own countries**, and no foreign agent acts at all.

⚠️ The coherent reading is layered: structure supplies the gradient, strategy maintains it — **largely dictated** names agency, the profit-margin asymmetry names structure. ⚠️ The chapter never states that reconciliation. 🔑 Verdict: structural in operation, strategic in maintenance, the source genuinely ambiguous.

**Rubric [15]:** the intentional definition quoted **3** · Nkrumah's agentive channels, at least three **2** · the structural explanation with its three attributes **3** · the margin asymmetry and the **continuously** adverb **2** · brain drain used as the agent-free case **2** · the layered reconciliation offered as a reading, with the admission that the chapter does not state it **2** · a graded verdict **1**.

---

## Question 4 [15 marks]

**Question:** Assess the chapter's claim that interdependence requires the concept of external sovereignty to be revised. *(Assess — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Model:** ✅ The claim rests on five questions the chapter says it is **natural to ask** once nation-states are interdependent **in their economic relations, environmentally and even emotionally**: how a nation can **claim absolute sovereignty in its external relations**; **what is the duty of developed nations toward promoting economic growth in the developing societies**; **at what point the issues of national importance end and those of international importance begin**; **how can needy nations lay hands on those essential and rare resources which are the exclusive monopoly of the few nations**; and **which nations are responsible to control worldwide terrorism, drug-trafficking and environmental degradation**. ⚠️ Each attacks a distinct attribute — absoluteness, freedom from external duty, a reserved domestic sphere, exclusive control of resources, and the territorial allocation of responsibility. ✅ The conclusion follows: **the concept of external sovereignty of nation-state will have to be revised suitably in order to deal with such situations**.

⚠️ Assessing it: the case is strong, because the five questions are unanswerable from the classical concept and two of them presuppose states as bearers of duty, so the concept is inadequate rather than false. ⚠️ The case is incomplete in three ways. ⚠️ **Suitably** specifies nothing. ⚠️ The chapter's own closing box records a realist view that globalization **does not transcend state sovereignty**, printed without correction. ⚠️ And the remedial injunction addresses an undefined **we** while the chapter's own finding is that power is **spread over various centres**. 🔑 Verdict: the demand for revision is earned; the revision itself is not supplied.

**Rubric [15]:** all five questions reported **5** · at least four attributes correctly identified **3** · the conclusion quoted exactly **2** · the observation that two questions presuppose states **2** · at least two of the three stated incompletenesses **2** · a graded verdict **1**.

---

## Question 5 [20 marks]

**Question:** "Each of Gauba's three challenges removes a different defence, and only the legal title survives all three." Discuss, and state what follows for the concept of sovereignty. *(Discuss — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Model:** ✅ The first challenge removed **self-rule**: empire brought **several countries under the control of one supreme authority**, for **the expansion of their trade and industry**. ✅ Decolonization returned it — **colonial rulers began to concede independence** — but not **economic self-determination**, since prices remained **largely dictated by the rich countries**, corporations earned **huge profits for the countries of their origin**, and professionals left as **'brain drain'**, so that **the legacy of imperialism still continues to afflict the developing countries who have gained formal, political independence**. ✅ The second removed **self-defence**: with **very big military alliances, with their own nuclear weapons and Intercontinental Ballistic Missiles (ICBMs)**, **no nation-state remained capable of providing for an effective security of its citizens by conventional means**, so each **felt constrained to rely on either superpower**, meaning **compromising one's independence and bowing to the commands of a superpower**. ✅ The third removed **insulation**: with domination's base **shifting from military power to economic power, spread over various centres**, a price, a particle, an image or a warning crosses any frontier.

✅ What survives is the title: a country may still declare itself **a 'sovereign, democratic republic'**, and the chapter's own last box records that globalization **does not transcend state sovereignty**. ⚠️ What follows is neither abolition nor complacency but the chapter's two demands: that the concept **be revised suitably**, and that **we must... maximize its benefits for humanity and prevent its damaging effects**. 🔑 A legal status intact, a capacity diminished, and a concept whose content is left open.

**Rubric [20]:** all four defences named and correctly assigned **4** · at least three neo-colonial mechanisms **3** · the persistence clause closing the first division **2** · the bloc argument with the universal incapacity clause and the two costs **4** · the hand-off with **spread over various centres** **2** · the surviving title, with the realist entry as corroboration **2** · both remedial demands quoted **2** · a closing formulation that avoids both extremes **1**.

---

## Question 6 [20 marks]

**Question:** "The chapter's only remedy for lost autonomy is joint action, and joint action is itself a constraint." Critically examine. *(Critically examine — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Model:** ✅ The first limb is supported throughout. ✅ Against price dictation, the only escape named is organisation **into a semi-cartel like the Organization of the Petroleum Exporting Countries (OPEC) (founded in 1960)**, with the immediate limit that **for other products, such organizations simply do not exist**. ✅ Against bloc pressure, the response was collective: non-alignment under **Nehru (of India), Nasser (of Egypt) and Tito (of Yugoslavia)** **evolved into a movement as well as a common forum**, with aims including **cooperation between the developing countries** and organisations for **industry and trade, technological development, education and information**. ✅ Against trade barriers, **groups of certain countries seek to eliminate the artificial barriers**, through five named forms, a process that **gives an impetus to globalization**. ✅ Below the state, subordinate groups were **inspired... to form their global organizations for their emancipation**.

✅ The second limb follows from the forms: each rung from **Preferential Trade Agreement** to **Economic Union** deepens a common discipline, and non-alignment imposed its own, its members accepting **criticism, pressures and threats from both side** to hold the common position.

⚠️ Three qualifications. ⚠️ The chapter never states this pattern and never recommends joint action; its prescription is the vaguer **devise suitable ways and means**. ⚠️ It assesses only one instance, the **semi-cartel**, and closes that route for most products. ⚠️ And it never asks whether a discipline accepted under severe constraint is accepted at all. 🔑 Verdict: the pattern is real and unacknowledged, and its difficulty — recovery through self-binding — is what the chapter does not analyse.

**Rubric [20]:** all three state-level instances with their textual evidence **6** · the sub-state instance **2** · the OPEC limitation clause **2** · the integration ladder used to show deepening discipline **3** · the non-alignment costs used as a second case of self-binding **2** · the observation that the chapter never states the pattern **2** · at least two further qualifications **2** · a graded verdict **1**.

---

# REMEDIATION

⚠️ Sixty errors, grouped by where they arise. ⚠️ Each row states the error as a candidate would actually commit it, and the correction in the source's own terms wherever the source supplies them.

## Set 1 — the boundary and the vocabulary (Lessons 1-5)

| # | ❌ Error | ✅ Correction |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | ❌ "This topic is about globalisation." | ✅ It is about **external challenges to sovereignty**; globalization is the process that **further aggravated the situation** |
| 2 | ❌ "The pluralist challenge and the globalisation challenge are two versions of one argument." | ✅ The chapter divides them by source: **within the state** against **from international environment** |
| 3 | ❌ "External challenges threatened the authority of the state." | ✅ They **threatened autonomy of the nation-state** — ⚠️ capacity, not competence |
| 4 | ❌ "Imperialism began in the nineteenth century." | ✅ **In this sense it has existed since ancient times**; only the modern form is dated |
| 5 | ❌ "Imperialism ended with decolonization." | ✅ **Apparently it has wound up, but the forces involved in its expansion continue to work in subtle and disguised forms** |
| 6 | ❌ "Colonialism and imperialism are the same thing." | ✅ Colonialism is **almost always a consequence of imperialism** and is **the implanting of settlements on distant territory** |
| 7 | ❌ "Gauba rejects Said's distinction." | ✅ He calls it **aptly drawn** and then records that **in actual practice... the term 'imperialism' is used more comprehensively** |
| 8 | ❌ Quoting Said as "rule from a metropolitan centre" only. | ✅ **The practice, the theory, and the attitudes** — ⚠️ the last two are what let imperialism outlive the empire |
| 9 | ❌ Writing the three divisions as three unconnected essays. | ✅ Use the chapter's hinges — **still continues**, **another factor**, **shifting** |
| 10 | ❌ "The chapter has three headings, all in the text." | ⚠️ Only **II. ROLE OF THE POWER BLOCS** is text; **I. LEGACY OF IMPERIALISM** and **III. PROCESS OF GLOBALIZATION** are embedded heading images |

## Set 2 — the legacy of imperialism (Lessons 6-13)

| # | ❌ Error | ✅ Correction |
|---|---|---|
| 11 | ❌ "Colonies were acquired while European states were still forming." | ✅ They were sought **after their own national consolidation** |
| 12 | ❌ "Imperialism was about raw materials." | ✅ **Natural and human resources** — both nouns, from the first causal paragraph |
| 13 | ❌ Repeating the civilising claim without the chapter's verdict. | ✅ **An attempt to attribute moral dignity to the pursuit of their self-interest** |
| 14 | ❌ Attributing "captive market" to Lenin. | ✅ **J.A. Hobson** (*Imperialism*; **1902**) |
| 15 | ❌ Attributing the necessity thesis to Hobson. | ✅ **Lenin** — **an economic necessity of capitalist economy** |
| 16 | ❌ Giving Lenin's drivers as "markets and raw materials". | ✅ Three: **new spheres of investment, new markets and new sources of raw materials** |
| 17 | ❌ "Lenin's essence of imperialism is class conflict." | ✅ The **division of nations into oppressor and oppressed** |
| 18 | ❌ "Lenin's call to unite succeeded." | ✅ It **could not go beyond Russian Revolution (1917) at that time**; struggles were launched **individually** |
| 19 | ❌ "Independence was seized." | ⚠️ The chapter writes that rulers **began to concede independence**, after struggle |
| 20 | ❌ "Neo-colonialism applies only to recently decolonised states." | ✅ The chapter includes **Latin America**, independent since **early nineteenth century** |
| 21 | ❌ Defining neo-colonialism as continued exploitation only. | ✅ Both limbs: it **does not maintain its political domination** **but continues its economic exploitation** |
| 22 | ❌ "Developing countries are poor because they lack resources." | ✅ They have **vast size and population**; the binding constraint is **low level of industrial development** |
| 23 | ❌ "Producers' organisations set world prices." | ✅ Prices are **largely dictated by the rich countries** **until** producers form a **semi-cartel**; ❌ **for other products, such organizations simply do not exist** |
| 24 | ❌ Stating adverse trade terms without the mechanism. | ✅ **Labour-intensive industries with a low margin of profit** against **capital-intensive and technology-intensive industries with a high margin of profit** |
| 25 | ❌ "Multinational corporations export raw materials." | ✅ They **manufacture and market... within those countries** and earn **huge profits for the countries of their origin** |
| 26 | ❌ Omitting the components channel. | ✅ Developing countries **serve as the provider of cheap labour for the production of components in consumer goods assembled elsewhere where requisite expertise is available** |
| 27 | ❌ Omitting brain drain. | ✅ **Doctors, engineers and other professionals, produced at a high public cost**, **directly transferred to the developed world** |
| 28 | ❌ "Nkrumah coined the term, full stop." | ⚠️ The chapter says **coined**; 🔶 both owners say **popularised**, citing earlier circulation; ✅ say that he gave the concept its canonical formulation in **1965** |
| 29 | ❌ Giving Nkrumah two or three channels. | ✅ Four: **international monetary bodies**; **the fixing of prices on world markets**; **multinational corporations and cartels**; **a variety of educational and cultural institutions** |
| 30 | ❌ "Formal independence ended the problem." | ✅ A **'sovereign, democratic republic' may still feel constrained by the forces of neo-colonialism** |

## Set 3 — the power blocs (Lessons 14-20)

| # | ❌ Error | ✅ Correction |
|---|---|---|
| 31 | ❌ Treating the chapter's NATO list as the founding signatories. | ⚠️ The chapter writes **leading members**; ⚠️ the Treaty was signed on **4 April 1949** by twelve states and the Federal Republic acceded on **6 May 1955** |
| 32 | ❌ Treating the Warsaw Pact list as complete. | ✅ The chapter writes **members included**, and records **Albania (which left it in 1968)** |
| 33 | ❌ "Superpower means a very large state." | ✅ **Capable of facing any big power with its large military might and nuclear weapons** |
| 34 | ❌ "The Cold War was a period." | ✅ A **queer conflict** with **no armed conflict... but a vigorous propaganda**, which **created immense tension** |
| 35 | ❌ "Lippman coined the term Cold War." | ✅ **Popularized**, and **in this sense** |
| 36 | ❌ "Sauvy coined 'third world'." | ✅ The countries were so described **at the instance of** **Alfred Sauvy (1898-1990)** |
| 37 | ❌ "Non-alignment was neutrality." | ✅ It was **the policy of remaining away from the power blocs** to **maintain their independence**, with four enumerated aims and a fifth undertaking — building **a bridge of understanding** — in the next sentence |
| 38 | ❌ Giving non-alignment no aims, or counting five inside the **aims included** sentence. | ✅ Four are enumerated there — including **disarmament for the maintenance of world peace** and organisations for **industry and trade, technological development, education and information** — and the fifth undertaking follows separately |
| 39 | ❌ "Small states could not defend themselves." | ✅ **No nation-state remained capable of providing for an effective security of its citizens by conventional means** |
| 40 | ❌ Omitting the old security model. | ✅ **Organizing its military power and... entering into alliance with other friendly states** |
| 41 | ❌ Giving bloc membership one cost. | ✅ Two: **compromising one's independence** and **bowing to the commands of a superpower** |
| 42 | ❌ "Non-alignment was the easy option." | ✅ **They really had a tough time, facing criticism, pressures and threats from both side** |
| 43 | ❌ "Non-alignment achieved disarmament and development." | ✅ Only that it **came to stay** and that these countries **largely maintained their independence throughout** |
| 44 | ❌ "1991 restored sovereignty." | ✅ **From military angle, only one power bloc is left now**, and the **substantive base of domination... has been shifting from military power to economic power** |
| 45 | ❌ Using the chapter's alliance statements in the present tense. | ⚠️ They are **2009**-edition statements; ⚠️ the only dated check in this file is the alliance's continued existence and its **32** members as of **7 March 2024** |

## Set 4 — the process of globalization (Lessons 21-25)

| # | ❌ Error | ✅ Correction |
|---|---|---|
| 46 | ❌ "Economic issues became important after the Cold War." | ✅ **In late 1970s and 1980s** — ⚠️ before **1991** |
| 47 | ❌ "Globalisation is purely economic." | ✅ Problems **may touch many areas but primarily they are centred around economic issues**; interdependence runs **environmentally and even emotionally** |
| 48 | ❌ Using the chapter's creditor, debtor or growth-centre statements as current facts. | ⚠️ All eight indicators are **2009**-edition reports |
| 49 | ❌ Treating globalization as a single idea. | 🔑 Two senses: a **condition** that emerged, and a **policy** that nations **adopted... as a conscious policy** |
| 50 | ❌ Presenting the five-step case for liberalization as the chapter's finding. | ⚠️ It is the rationale the chapter reports; its own critics reject at least the claims that the collapse **proved** a general proposition and that welfare failed **due to bureaucratic inefficiency and corruption** |

## Set 5 — instruments, appraisal and perspectives (Lessons 26-32)

| # | ❌ Error | ✅ Correction |
|---|---|---|
| 51 | ❌ "Structural adjustment is globalisation." | ✅ **A process closely linked with globalization** — ⚠️ adjacent, not identical |
| 52 | ❌ Giving structural adjustment one class of measure. | ✅ Two: **eliminate inefficiency in the use of resources** and speed **adjustment to technological innovations and changes in relative prices**; and **increase output potential by adding to productive resources... or by raising overall productivity** |
| 53 | ❌ Quoting the lending box as the chapter's verdict. | ⚠️ The same page also records terms **dictated by International Monetary Fund (IMF), World Bank and other Western-dominated agencies**; ❌ neither register is the settled verdict |
| 54 | ❌ Describing conditional lending as delegated sovereignty. | 🔶 The owners classify it as **a consented but often unequal external constraint**, expressly **not** delegation; ⚠️ and that vocabulary is the owners', ❌ not Gauba's |
| 55 | ❌ Defining the rungs of the integration ladder. | ❌ The chapter defines none; ➡️ their technical content belongs to the Economy and International Relations owners |
| 56 | ❌ "Regional integration is an alternative to globalisation." | ✅ **This process gives an impetus to globalization** |
| 57 | ❌ Presenting the four positive claims as Gauba's own position. | ✅ They are what **some writers eulogize**, matched by what **others condemn** |
| 58 | ❌ Omitting the distributive half of the agencies allegation. | ✅ The terms **result in benefiting a tiny class within the country at the expense of the large majority** |
| 59 | ❌ Treating "disintegrated communities" and "liberated them from the ideological domination of their local communities" as a contradiction. | ⚠️ One effect, two valuations, depending on one's position inside the community |
| 60 | ❌ Closing an answer with "sovereignty has been pooled and delegated". | ⚠️ That vocabulary is the owners' analytical addition; ✅ the chapter's own last word is the realist entry that globalization **does not transcend state sovereignty**, beside its demand that the concept **be revised suitably** |

# MASTER COMPARISON, CAUSAL AND ARGUMENT MAPS

## Map 1 — the four removals, and what each leaves standing

| ⚠️ Defence | ✅ Removed by | ✅ The chapter's evidence | ⚠️ Restored? | ⚠️ What still stands afterwards |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Self-rule** | Imperial and colonial rule | **Bringing several countries under the control of one supreme authority**; colonies sought **for the expansion of their trade and industry after their own national consolidation** | ✅ Yes — **colonial rulers began to concede independence**; **India... first... in 1947** | ⚠️ Economic organisation built for another economy |
| **Economic self-determination** | Neo-colonial mechanisms | Prices **largely dictated by the rich countries**; **low margin** against **high margin**; MNC profits **for the countries of their origin**; components **assembled elsewhere**; **'brain drain'** | ❌ No — **the legacy of imperialism still continues to afflict the developing countries** | ⚠️ Formal, political independence |
| **Self-defence** | Power blocs | **Very big military alliances, with their own nuclear weapons and Intercontinental Ballistic Missiles (ICBMs)**; **no nation-state remained capable** | ⚠️ Partly protected by non-alignment, at the cost of **criticism, pressures and threats from both side** | ⚠️ Political independence, defended at a price |
| **Insulation** | Globalization | Base of domination **shifting from military power to economic power, spread over various centres**; pollution; **effective communication network**; interdependence **even emotionally** | ❌ No — stopping the process **would be futile** | 🔑 The legal title: **a 'sovereign, democratic republic'** |

## Map 3 — four compulsions, by who compels

| ⚠️ Mechanism | ⚠️ Compeller | ⚠️ Visible as domination? | ⚠️ Resistance available |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Imperial rule** | An identified foreign power | ✅ Yes | ✅ **Struggle for independence**, launched **individually** |
| **Neo-colonial dependence** | ⚠️ Partly agents (**largely dictated**), partly structure (**low level of industrial development**) | ⚠️ **New, subtle means** | ⚠️ Collective producer organisation — available once, closed elsewhere |
| **Bloc pressure** | Two identified superpowers | ✅ Yes — **bowing to the commands** | ✅ **Non-alignment**, at a cost |
| **Globalization** | ❌ Nobody — power **spread over various centres**; manufacturers **were forced** with no one forcing | ❌ No | ⚠️ None named; the chapter substitutes management for resistance |

## Map 5 — three triads that repeat across a century

| ⚠️ | **Lenin, 1916** | **Neo-colonial definition** | **Globalization as policy** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Source of profit abroad | **New spheres of investment** | ⚠️ MNC capital investment | **Capital and machinery available in one part of the world** |
| Material input | **New sources of raw materials** | **A source of... raw materials** | **Raw materials in another part** |
| Human input | **New sources of labour** | **A source of cheap labour** | **Labour in still another part** |
| Outlet | **New markets** | **A big market for its industrial products** | **Search of markets for the final product all over the world** |
| Stated as | ⚠️ A compulsion on capitalists | ⚠️ A strategy of a colonial power | ⚠️ A policy adopted by governments |

🔑 The same four-element structure appears as a compulsion, as an imposition and finally as a programme a state adopts for itself. ⚠️ That progression — from something done to a country to something a country does — is the chapter's most striking unstated finding, and it is what makes the radical perspective's claim that globalization is **the latest stage** more than a slogan.

## Map 6 — the chapter's unresolved pairs

| ⚠️ Pair | ✅ One side | ✅ The other | ⚠️ Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| Lending | **Assistance on concessional terms... to enable** | Terms **dictated by... Western-dominated agencies** | ⚠️ Both printed on PDF p.218; not adjudicated |
| Culture | A **global culture which is expected to create greater understanding** | **Western culture... projected as global culture**, eroding **the cultural heritage of the East** | ⚠️ Expectation and denial, both reported |
| Community | **Disintegrated communities** | **Liberated them from the ideological domination of their local communities** | ⚠️ One effect, two valuations |
| Inevitability | Entrapment **by projecting the inevitability of globalization** | **When globalization became inevitable**; stopping it **would be futile** | ⚠️ The objection targets a claim the chapter makes |
| Poverty | **Failed to eradicate poverty... or reduce economic disparities** | **Reduced the ratio of the extremely poor in world population to a half** | ⚠️ Compatible, if read exactly |
| Sovereignty | The concept **will have to be revised suitably** | Globalization **does not transcend state sovereignty** | ⚠️ Reconcilable: the system survives, the concept does not survive unchanged |
| Cartels | A producers' **semi-cartel** as the only escape from price dictation | **Multinational corporations and cartels** as a channel of decisive influence | ⚠️ Not reconciled by the chapter |

# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

## Chapter identity, range and reading conditions

- ✅ Gauba, *An Introduction to Political Theory*, Fifth Edition **2009**, **Chapter 9 — Contemporary Challenges to Sovereignty**.
- ✅ **PDF pp.209-219 = printed pp.192-201.** ⚠️ Both canonical owners declare pp.209-218; the chapter's terminal page is **p.219**, carrying the running head and folio **201**.
- ✅ Offset **17** on PDF pp.209-214; offset **18** on PDF pp.216-219.
- ✅ **PDF p.215** carries no folio, no running head and no heading: a narrow single-column overflow reading **and Hong Kong. When American consumers purchased less**, bridging p.214 and p.216 with no loss.
- ✅ Three divisions: **I. Legacy of Imperialism** (image heading, p.209); **II. Role of the Power Blocs** (text, p.212); **III. Process of Globalization** (image heading, p.214).
- ✅ Four boxes: **Liberalization** and **Privatization** (p.217); **Process of Globalization** — structural adjustment and regional economic integration (p.218); **Perspectives on Globalization** (p.219).

## The chapter's own division of the problem

- ✅ The conventional theory **upholds supreme legal authority of the state in internal as well as external sphere**.
- ✅ **Internal challenges... came from the growing authority of various groups and associations who claimed allegiance from their members within the state** — **brought out by the pluralist theory of sovereignty**. ➡️ Topic 11.
- ✅ **External challenges to sovereignty came from international environment which threatened autonomy of the nation-state.**
- ✅ **The process of globalization further aggravated the situation.**
- ✅ **Here we shall focus on the external challenges to the sovereignty of the nation-state.**
- 🔑 The operative noun is **autonomy**: capacity, not legal competence.

## Imperialism — definition and genus

- ✅ **Imperialism has a long history. Apparently it has wound up, but the forces involved in its expansion continue to work in subtle and disguised forms.**
- ✅ **Originally imperialism meant the formation of an empire, that is bringing several countries under the control of one supreme authority.** ✅ **In this sense it has existed since ancient times.**
- ✅ **In modern times it found a new expression in the form of 'colonialism'.**
- ✅ **Edward Said** (*Culture and Imperialism*; **1993**), **aptly drawn**: imperialism = **the practice, the theory, and the attitudes of a dominating metropolitan centre ruling a distant territory**; colonialism = **almost always a consequence of imperialism** = **the implanting of settlements on distant territory**.
- ✅ **In actual practice, however, the term 'imperialism' is used more comprehensively. It includes various expressions of 'colonialism' also.**

## Causes, alibi and theories

- ✅ **Modern forms of imperialism are the product of expansion of trade and industry in several countries of Europe.**
- ✅ **Eighteenth and nineteenth centuries**; **Britain, France, Belgium, Netherlands, Spain and Portugal, among others**; colonies in **Africa, Asia and America**; **after their own national consolidation**.
- ✅ **They started exploiting the natural and human resources of those countries to strengthen their own economies.**
- ✅ The alibi: **the benefit of civilization to the 'uncivilized' people** — ✅ **an attempt to attribute moral dignity to the pursuit of their self-interest**.
- ✅ **J.A. Hobson** (*Imperialism*; **1902**): imperialism as **a search for 'captive market'**; **he attacked on it as a method of exploitation**.
- ✅ **V.I. Lenin** (*Imperialism: The Highest Stage of Capitalism*; **1916**): **an economic necessity of capitalist economy**; when capital **cannot at all find profitable use in the main capitalist countries, they are forced to look abroad for profitable outlets**.
- 🔑 Three driving forces: **new spheres of investment**, **new markets**, **new sources of raw materials**.
- ✅ **Foreign trade allows capitalists to secure a higher rate of return than that available at home**; overseas investment **opens up new sources of labour and markets** and gives **access to raw materials at extremely cheaper rates**.
- ✅ **Division of nations into oppressor and oppressed** = **the essence of imperialism**; the call to unite **could not go beyond Russian Revolution (1917) at that time**.

## Decolonization

- ✅ The oppressed nations **gradually became aware of the nature of their exploitation** and **launched their struggle for independence individually**.
- ✅ **The process of 'decolonization' began after the Second World War (1939-45) when colonial rulers began to concede independence to their subject countries.**
- ✅ **India was first to gain independence in 1947.**

## Neo-colonialism — definition and mechanisms

- ✅ **The exploitation of the former colonies of Asia, Africa and Latin America did not stop with the achievement of their independence.**
- ✅ **The former imperialist powers acquired new, subtle means... described as 'neo-colonialism'.**
- 🔑 **Neo-colonialism denotes the strategy of a colonial power which does not maintain its political domination in a foreign territory, but continues its economic exploitation by using it as a source of cheap labour and raw materials as well as a big market for its industrial products.**
- ✅ Structural reason: **vast size and population and low level of industrial development** keep them **the chief source of supply of raw materials and labour-intensive products**.
- ✅ **The price levels for such products are largely dictated by the rich countries until they can organize themselves into a semi-cartel like the Organization of the Petroleum Exporting Countries (OPEC) (founded in 1960).** ❌ **For other products, such organizations simply do not exist.**
- ✅ **Labour-intensive industries with a low margin of profit** against **capital-intensive and technology-intensive industries with a high margin of profit**; **thus international trade continuously operates to the disadvantage of the developing countries**.
- ✅ Book-period instances: **Chile, Brazil, Panama** for United States industry; **Taiwan, Malaysia and Philippines** as **backyards for the Japanese industries**.
- ✅ **MNCs... manufacture and market a variety of consumers goods within those countries and earn huge profits for the countries of their origin.**
- ✅ Components: developing countries **serve as the provider of cheap labour for the production of components in consumer goods assembled elsewhere where requisite expertise is available**; they **lack the expertise or investment capital to benefit from the marketing of finished products**; factories are **owned and built by companies based in richer countries**.
- ✅ **Brain drain**: **doctors, engineers and other professionals, produced at a high public cost**, **directly transferred to the developed world for want of adequate career opportunities in their own countries**.

## Nkrumah

- ✅ **Kwame Nkrumah (1909-72)**, **the first President of independent Ghana**; *Neo-colonialism: The Last Stage of Imperialism* (**1965**), described by the chapter as an **essay**.
- ✅ The work **follows the style and line of argument of Lenin's *Imperialism: The Highest Stage of Capitalism* (1916)**.
- 🔑 Four channels of continuing decisive influence: **international monetary bodies**; **the fixing of prices on world markets**; **multinational corporations and cartels**; **a variety of educational and cultural institutions**.
- ✅ **More injurious and more difficult to detect than the conventional form of colonialism.**
- ⚠️ The chapter says **coined**; 🔶 both canonical owners say **popularised**, citing earlier circulation; ❌ neither is adopted here.
- ✅ Division verdict: **the legacy of imperialism still continues to afflict the developing countries who have gained formal, political independence**; a **'sovereign, democratic republic' may still feel constrained by the forces of neo-colonialism**.

## The power blocs

- ✅ **Another factor that caused a constraint on the sovereignty of the developing countries**; yet those countries **largely maintained their independence throughout**.
- ✅ **Two big ideological camps**: **capitalist camp led by the United States**; **communist camp led by the Soviet Union (the then USSR)**.
- 🔑 Superpower test: **capable of facing any big power with its large military might and nuclear weapons**.
- ✅ **NATO**, **founded in 1949**; **leading members**: the United States, the United Kingdom, France, Belgium, Netherlands, **Luxemberg**, Denmark, Norway, Iceland, Portugal, Italy, **West Germany (Federal Republic of Germany)**, Canada.
- ✅ **Warsaw Pact**, **founded in 1955**; **members included**: the USSR, **Albania (which left it in 1968)**, Bulgaria, Czechoslovakia, **East Germany (German Democratic Republic)**, Hungary, Poland, **Rumania**.
- ⚠️ The chapter's nouns are **leading members** and **members included**; ⚠️ the Treaty was signed on **4 April 1949** by twelve states and the Federal Republic acceded on **6 May 1955**.
- ✅ **These were termed the 'power blocs'** because both camps **sought to dominate the world with their huge power apparatus**; each wanted **to demonstrate the supremacy of its ideology and to enlist its following in the rest of the world**.
- ✅ **There was no armed conflict between the two groups, but a vigorous propaganda against each other was in full swing**; this **queer conflict** was the **'Cold War'**, which **created immense tension and threatened the world peace**; the term **in this sense was popularized by the American columnist Walter Lippman (1889-1974)**.

## The third world and non-alignment

- ✅ **The emergence of the power blocs coincided with the decline of imperialism.**
- ✅ Latin American states, independent since **early nineteenth century**, **also joined them**.
- ✅ The group **had remained underdeveloped during the long period of foreign domination**, and were **chiefly interested in maintaining their independence and securing their social and economic development**.
- ✅ **'Third world'** **at the instance of the French economist and demographer Alfred Sauvy (1898-1990)**.
- ✅ Under **Nehru (of India), Nasser (of Egypt) and Tito (of Yugoslavia)**, **the policy of remaining away from the power blocs** — **'non-alignment'** — became **a distinctive feature of the third world** and **evolved into a movement as well as a common forum for the new nations**.
- 🔑 **Four** enumerated aims: **opposition to colonialism and neo-colonialism all over the world**; **disarmament for the maintenance of world peace**; **cooperation between the developing countries**; and organisations for common interests in **industry and trade, technological development, education and information**. ⚠️ A **fifth** undertaking is stated in the following sentence: **to dispel the tension between the two power blocs and to build a bridge of understanding between the two**.

## What the blocs did, and how they ended

- ✅ Old model: **any state could arrange for its defence by organizing its military power and by entering into alliance with other friendly states**.
- ✅ New condition: **very big military alliances, with their own nuclear weapons and Intercontinental Ballistic Missiles (ICBMs)**.
- 🔑 **No nation-state remained capable of providing for an effective security of its citizens by conventional means.**
- ✅ **Each nation-state felt constrained to rely on either superpower and to join its camp**, meaning **compromising one's independence and bowing to the commands of a superpower**.
- ✅ Non-alignment's cost: **they really had a tough time, facing criticism, pressures and threats from both side. But eventually this came to stay.**
- ✅ **Reforms initiated by Mikhail Gorbachev in the Soviet Union in 1985** encouraged **free flow of information, social and political debates, decentralization of economy and review of foreign policy**.
- ✅ Then **relaxation of the Soviet Union's control over the communist nations of Eastern Europe**, and **eventual collapse of the communist system in most of these countries by 1991**.
- ✅ **Dissolution of the one power bloc led by the former USSR and consequent end of the 'cold war'**; **the Wall of Berlin... was demolished, and Warsaw Pact also became redundant**; **however, the North Atlantic Treaty Organization (NATO) is still going strong**; **from military angle, only one power bloc is left now**.
- 📰 Dated status check, carried once in this file: **Finland acceded on 4 April 2023 as the 31st member and Sweden on 7 March 2024 as the 32nd**, on the alliance's own official record. ❌ No doctrine depends on it.

## The hand-off, and the primacy of economic issues

- 🔑 **The substantive base of domination in the contemporary world has been shifting from military power to economic power, spread over various centres.**
- ✅ **The use of superior technology has led to the spreading of environmental pollution all over the world, and linking of all parts of the world by an effective communication network.**
- ✅ **In short, the process of 'globalization' has emerged as a new challenge to the sovereignty of the nation-state.**
- ✅ **The emerging problems of global concern and magnitude call for global solution**; they **may touch many areas but primarily they are centred around economic issues**.
- ✅ For **most of the 1950s, 1960s and early 1970s**, **lethal weapons... were regarded the symbols of its power**; **in late 1970s and 1980s economic issues began to acquire greater importance in international politics**.

## Eight signs of one world economy

- ✅ **The United States, Western Europe and Japan had become dependent on foreign sources of energy and other non-fuel mineral resources.**
- ✅ **Japan had emerged as a major international economic actor.**
- ✅ **The United States was reduced from the world's greatest creditor nation to the world's greatest debtor nation.**
- ✅ **The Soviet Union and Eastern European states conceded that they too had immense budget deficits.**
- ✅ **Western Europe decided to create a single European Common Market.**
- ✅ **Third world countries were groaning under a heavy debt burden**, with **large segments... still wanting in economic development**.
- ✅ **The centre of international economic growth had shifted to the western Pacific.**
- ✅ **A large part of the world had been transformed into a single financial market thanks to the development of electronic banking.**

## Traceable interdependence

- ✅ **Iraq conquered Kuwait in 1990**; **many countries feared that their economies would be hard hit**.
- ✅ The aircraft chain: America → **Japanese businessmen to Indonesia to secure oil** → **French financiers... London, New York, Tokyo and Hong Kong**.
- ✅ The textile chain: cheaper **East Asian** textiles → U.S. manufacturers **forced to lay off workers** → **high-technology West German textile machines** → bought with **money... deposited by Saudi princes in Italian banks**.
- ✅ Japanese electronics → **prices of VCRs, TVs and microwave ovens fall in America**.
- ✅ **Chernobyl... (1986)** → fear of contamination of **the food chain over half a world**.
- ✅ **World Trade Centre... (2001)** → **whole world was shocked while watching its live telecast on TV** (PDF p.216, printed 198).
- ✅ **Famine... Africa** → **TV viewers around the world are touched**.
- ✅ **Burning of forests in Indonesia brings climate change in whole South East Asia.**
- ✅ **A hole in the Earth's ozone layer over Antarctica** → **a warning of global disaster for the chemical industries world over**.

## The five questions and the demand

- ✅ Premise: nation-states are interdependent **in their economic relations, environmentally and even emotionally**.
- ✅ Q1 **How can a particular nation claim absolute sovereignty in its external relations?**
- ✅ Q2 **What is the duty of developed nations toward promoting economic growth in the developing societies?**
- ✅ Q3 **At what point the issues of national importance end and those of international importance begin?**
- ✅ Q4 **How can needy nations lay hands on those essential and rare resources which are the exclusive monopoly of the few nations?**
- ✅ Q5 **Which nations are responsible to control worldwide terrorism, drug-trafficking and environmental degradation?**
- 🔑 **The concept of external sovereignty of nation-state will have to be revised suitably in order to deal with such situations.**

## Globalization as a conscious policy

- ✅ **When globalization became inevitable, many nations... thought of finding out its beneficial aspects and adopting it as a conscious policy.**
- ✅ Five steps into liberalization: **governments of USA and UK** reduce **their liability of public welfare** in the **1980s**; the Eastern European collapse **proved that state-sponsored development could not sustain an economy for long**; third world countries found welfare **had failed to serve its purpose due to bureaucratic inefficiency and corruption**; **increasing tax-burden on able and industrious sections led to the loss of incentive and consequent decline of output**; **the mounting demands of subsidies could not be met with the available resources**. ✅ **So liberalization became order of the day.**
- ✅ **Liberalization**: **the policy of removal or relaxation of government control or restriction on economic activity with a view to maximizing its efficiency**; it **seeks to reduce the state's liability toward welfare of the individuals and promote incentive-based rewards rather than dependence on various subsidies**.
- ✅ **Privatization**: **the policy of transfering government ownership and control of any sector of economy to private ownership and control in order to improve the standard of its management and to prevent loss to the public exchequer**.
- 🔑 **Policy of globalization represents a logical conclusion of the policy of liberalization and privatization.**
- ✅ Production formula: **capital and machinery available in one part of the world, raw materials in another part, labour in still another part**, and **search of markets for the final product all over the world**; objectives — **minimization of cost**, **enhancement of its quality**, **maximization of profit**.
- ✅ Four consequences: MNC expansion **because they are best-suited to mobilize capital and other resources including managerial skills**; **expansion of transport and communication network**; **standard goods and services to every nook and cranny of the world**, hence **uniform style of living and uniform ways of entertainment**; and **a global culture which is expected to create greater understanding among different peoples of the world**.

## The two boxed instruments

- ✅ **Structural Adjustment**: **a process closely linked with globalization**; **reforms that enhance productive potential and facilitate improvement in economic performance and standard of living**; **International Monetary Fund (IMF) provides assistance on concessional terms to low-income countries to enable them to carry out such reforms**; measures **(a)** that **eliminate inefficiency in the use of resources and allow more rapid adjustment to technological innovations and changes in relative prices**, and **(b)** that **increase output potential by adding to productive resources (such as capital and labour) or by raising overall productivity**.
- ✅ **Regional Economic Integration**: **the process whereby groups of certain countries seek to eliminate the artificial barriers in the way of international trade and competition**, **largely accomplished by (a) Preferential Trade Agreement; (b) Free Trade Area; (c) Customs Union; (d) Common Market; and (e) Economic Union**; **this process gives an impetus to globalization**.

## The critical appraisal

- ✅ **Some writers eulogize globalization as a boon for humanity**: **national boundaries do not stand in the way of progress of an individual or a community**; **men have gained access to the treasure of knowledge and culture which is the product of genius all over the world**; **local communities have the opportunity to benefit from technology, information services and markets available anywhere in the world**; globalization **has created awareness regarding global environment all over the world**, and **different nations have come to recognize global problems as a matter of their individual and collective responsibility**.
- ✅ **Others condemn globalization as a bane for a large part of the world**: it is **a device to maintain domination of the developed countries of the West over the developing countries**; **an expression of neo-colonialism**; **Western culture is being projected as global culture through the so-called globalization, and thereby the cultural heritage of the East is being eroded**; **local economies are being made subservient to Western economies by linking them with each other in the name of globalization**.
- ✅ **Global culture and global economy have not come into existence in the course of natural evolution, but they have been invented by capitalist powers to serve their self-interest**; **underdeveloped countries are being entrapped by projecting the inevitability of globalization**.
- ✅ **Some critics allege** that globalization implies **acceptance of terms and conditions dictated by International Monetary Fund (IMF), World Bank and other Western-dominated agencies which result in benefiting a tiny class within the country at the expense of the large majority**.

## The balance and the verdict

- ✅ Six conceded harms: **failed to eradicate poverty from the world or reduce economic disparities**; **environmental degradation**; **prompted intolerance**; **encouraged militarism**; **disintegrated communities**; **worsened the condition of subordinate groups**.
- ✅ Five gains: **raised real per capita income in the world to three times since 1945**; **reduced the ratio of the extremely poor in world population to a half**; **created awareness regarding environment**; **congenial conditions for disarmament**; and, for subordinate groups, **brought... to limelight**, **inspired them to form their global organizations for their emancipation**, and **liberated them from the ideological domination of their local communities and enabled them to fight for their legitimate rights**.
- 🔑 **On the whole, any attempt to stop the process of globalization in the present-day scenario would be futile. We must seriously analyse its beneficial and harmful aspects and devise suitable ways and means to maximize its benefits for humanity and prevent its damaging effects.**

## Perspectives on globalization

- ✅ **Realist**: globalization **does not alter the existing territorial division of world into nation-states**; it **increases interdependence of different societies and economies but it does not interfere with the state-system**; **it does not transcend state sovereignty, nor the struggle for political power between states**.
- ✅ **Liberal**: it **has transformed world politics by replacing states as central actors by a myriad of other groups and organizations whose importance differs according to the issue-area concerned**; through **revolution in technology and communications interlinking economies of different nations**, it introduced **a new international division of labour, bringing greater efficiency, choice and prosperity**.
- ✅ **Radical**: it is **nothing but the latest stage in the development of international capitalism**; **a Western-led phenomenon which further deepens the existing divide between the metropolitan and peripheral economies**; **at best it is an instrument of neo-colonialism**.

## 🔶 Owner additions, with their cautions

- 🔶 **Pooling**, **delegation**, and **consented but often unequal external constraint** — the Core owner's three-way distinction, with conditional lending classified as the third and expressly **not** delegation; ⚠️ analytical, ❌ not in Gauba.
- 🔶 **Hyperglobalist / sceptic / transformationalist** — cautiously associated with **David Held and co-authors, *Global Transformations*, 1999**; the owner reads Gauba as **closest to a transformationalist position without using that label**; ⚠️ for use **only as an organising lens**, ❌ never as part of Gauba's argument.
- 🔶 Mark-scaled architecture: **10** — thesis, one mechanism, one named example, verdict; **15** — add a second mechanism, the Nkrumah correction, an objection-reply pair; **20** — the full three-part spine, an organising frame, a cautious application, a verdict distinguishing legal persistence from practical constraint.
- 🔶 India: the **1991** balance-of-payments crisis and subsequent liberalisation may be used **only as a general, widely known historical fact**; ❌ no figures, dates, clauses or trade-policy specifics; ❌ Gauba's edition does not detail the Indian case.

## Five things the chapter leaves open

1. ⚠️ What a **suitably revised** concept would contain.
2. ⚠️ Who the managing **we** is, given power **spread over various centres**.
3. ⚠️ Whether consent under severe constraint is consent.
4. ⚠️ Whether a global culture creates understanding or erodes heritage.
5. ⚠️ Whether the three divisions share a mechanism or only a subject.

## Dated-anchor control

📰 **One dated teaching anchor is introduced at Lesson 19; its provenance is repeated only in the register notes and this ledger.** 📰 On the alliance's own official membership record, **Finland deposited its instrument of accession to the North Atlantic Treaty on 4 April 2023, becoming the 31st member country**, and **Sweden deposited its instrument of accession on 7 March 2024, becoming the 32nd member country**.

⚠️ It is used solely as a dated status check on the chapter's own present-tense sentence at PDF p.214, where **the North Atlantic Treaty Organization (NATO) is still going strong**. ❌ It is not evidence for any claim in the chapter. ❌ It is not presented as endorsing the chapter. ❌ It is not a globalisation argument; ➡️ the contemporary international order, alliance politics and foreign policy belong to the **International Relations** owners. ❌ No doctrine, lesson, concept check, Mains model or application anchor in this file depends on it. ❌ No second current linkage appears anywhere in this file, and every other lesson records a truthful nil in its pre-teach checklist.

⚠️ **One bounded historical cross-check is carried separately, at Lesson 14**, from the same official record: the North Atlantic Treaty was signed on **4 April 1949** by twelve states, and the Federal Republic of Germany acceded on **6 May 1955**. ⚠️ It is a verification of a historical membership question raised by the chapter's own wording, ❌ not a current-affairs anchor, and ❌ nothing in this file's argument depends on it either.

❌ **No other post-2009 substantive, statistical, statutory or policy fact is introduced anywhere in this file.** ⚠️ Exempted from that statement: bibliographic forms, examination years and paper codes, which are bibliographic rather than substantive, and the standard-form corrections catalogued in the normalisations table.
